MFB50 is the crank angle by which half the fuel has burned. At the best timing it lands between 8 and 10 degrees after top dead centre in almost any engine — which makes it the single best check that a combustion model is behaving. Ours gives 8.5° at 2500 rpm and 60 kPa, which is the row validate.py prints and marks as inside the band.

4 · Lambda (λ) and Enrichment

Lambda (λ) compares the air the engine actually takes in with the air it would need to burn its fuel completely. The exact ratio for petrol is 14.7 parts air to one part fuel by mass, and it is called stoichiometric. λ = 1 means exactly enough; λ below 1 is a rich mixture with surplus fuel; λ above 1 is a lean mixture with surplus air.

Enrichment is deliberately running the engine rich at high load. Thermodynamically it is plain waste: you inject fuel you know will not burn. Every manufacturer does it anyway.

There are three reasons, and they add up. First, liquid fuel evaporates inside the cylinder, and evaporating takes heat out of the charge — the air and fuel trapped in the cylinder. In a direct-injection engine, one that sprays fuel straight into the cylinder rather than into the inlet port, that cools the charge by about 25 K. Second, the surplus fuel is extra mass to heat up, so the same energy reaches a lower temperature (section 7 explains why mass slows temperature down). Third, the carbon monoxide left by incomplete combustion leaves through the exhaust carrying chemical energy that never became heat at all.

The conclusion to keep: enrichment is a thermal tool, and it is the fastest lever available when the turbo approaches its limit. That sentence alone explains why lambda is one of the agent's five levers.

Here is the project's own result on this car, from 1055 samples taken above 180 kPa across nine drives. The mixture gets richer — lambda falls — as engine speed rises (correlation −0.56), as air mass flow rises (−0.49), and the longer the engine has been held continuously above 180 kPa (−0.47). Each correlation is negative because a richer mixture is a lower lambda. We call that 180 kPa the enrichment gate (ENR_LOAD). Above it, the engine starts counting the seconds it has spent under high pressure, and we use that count as a stand-in for the turbine temperature this car never publishes.

Manifold pressure, meanwhile, correlates at only +0.23 — a weak signal, and pointing the wrong way: higher pressure comes with a leaner mixture, not a richer one. So the mixture on this car is not set from a load table, a lookup indexed by load alone. It is component protection.

5 · The Turbocharger and MAP

A turbocharger recycles energy that was about to be thrown away. Exhaust gas leaving at speed spins a wheel called the turbine. The turbine drives a second wheel on the same shaft, on the intake side: the compressor.

The compressor crams more air into the cylinder. More air means more fuel can be burnt, which means more power out of the same displacement. That is the whole idea.

The wastegate routes part of the exhaust around the turbine instead of through it, and it is what decides how much boost — pressure above atmospheric — you actually get. Without it, the compressor would keep raising pressure until something broke.

The measurement that matters to us is MAP — manifold absolute pressure, in kilopascals: the air pressure in the pipe that feeds the cylinders. Below atmospheric you are in vacuum; above it you are in boost. Atmospheric pressure on this car reads about 98 kPa (14.23 psi), and the model uses 99.3 kPa rather than the textbook 101.

MAP is the main load axis of every engine map you will see here — the tables that set spark and mixture against speed and load. But always state its limit in the same breath: MAP is an input to this model, not something the model predicts. validate.py says so at the end of every run. Peak power is therefore not a prediction, because the pressure is the thing we command in the first place. That closing note also says the model has no ceiling at all, and there it is out of date — it predates the one we added. What holds the pressure down today is plant.boost_ceiling_kpa, and that is simply the highest pressure we saw the car reach at each speed: not a real compressor map with efficiency contours and shaft-speed curves.

One warning before you use any of this. A channel is one named signal the car publishes through its diagnostic port, and the channel that calls itself MAP on this car is not MAP at all. That is why we work the pressure out from air flow instead of reading it directly. The detail is in the table at the foot of this page.

6 · EGT, the turbine housing, and what "damage" means

EGT — exhaust gas temperature — is the temperature of the gas at the moment it leaves the cylinder. In our model it is 887 °C at 3000 rpm and 180 kPa with knock-limited spark at λ = 0.92, the load-scheduled mixture check_map.py uses there. Run the model through a lambda sweep at the same speed and load — mixture varied step by step, spark fixed at 14 °BTDC — and it reaches 937 °C at λ = 1.00 and falls to 817 °C at λ = 0.75.

Two trends to know by heart: exhaust temperature rises as you retard the spark, and falls as you enrich the mixture. The two numbers above are that cooling in plain sight — 120 °C removed by going from λ = 1.00 to λ = 0.75. That is why spark and lambda are thermal levers, not just torque levers.

The limit we use in every experiment is not a gas temperature at all. It is the metal temperature of the turbine housing: 1123 K, about 850 °C. We did not take it from a handbook and we did not pick it by feel — it is the knee of the damage function exp((T − 1123) ÷ 45), the bend where modelled damage stops being negligible and starts climbing steeply with every further degree. Every file reads it from engine_env.TURB_PROTECT_K, so two experiments cannot quietly disagree about the same limit.

The turbine housing is the thing this project protects, and we chose it for two reasons. Its temperature is the dominant variable in the damage model, and its 48-second time constant is close to the 30 seconds of road we can see ahead. Comparing those two numbers is exactly what H/τ does.

But be precise about what "damage" means, because an examiner is certain to ask. The numbers check_premise.py prints — 829.2, 548.6, 437.6 — are one quantity integrated over time, built from three terms: exp((T_turb − 1123)/45) for the turbine housing, 0.4·exp((T_oil − 408)/12) for the oil, and 40·max(0, KI − 0.85)² for knock.

The turbine-housing term dominates, and the threshold was derived from it, but oil and knock enter at a lower weight — the script prints a "peak oil C" column of its own. Torque is not a protected component: it is a hard constraint inside the reward, the score the agent is trained to maximise. Only the H/τ sweep in generality_test.py scores turbine temperature on its own.

And the most important admission in this section: turbine housing temperature is computed, not measured. This car publishes no turbine temperature at all. The only exhaust channel it offers is "exhaust temperature after catalytic converter, modelled" — modelled, meaning the ECU calculates rather than measures it, and taken downstream of the catalytic converter that cleans the exhaust. It cannot check our turbine temperature. So our damage figure is a model output we build on, not metal wear anybody measured.

7 · Thermal mass, the time constant τ, and the preview horizon H

This is the central quantity of the whole project. If you remember one thing from this page, make it this.

We treat each piece of metal as a single mass at a single temperature, and the simplification is called lumped capacitance. The equation is one line: C·dT/dt = Qin − UA·(T − Tamb).

What it means in words: C is the thermal mass, how much energy it takes to raise the part by one degree — a heavy part has a large C. UA is how much heat leaks out of the part into its surroundings per degree of difference — better cooling means a larger UA. Tamb is the ambient temperature, and Qin the heat coming in. So the whole line says: heat in, minus heat leaking out, is what is left to warm the part — and the more that is left, the faster it warms.

That equation gives the time constant, τ = C ÷ UA. In practice it means this: how long the part takes to cover 63 % of the distance to its new temperature. And 63 % is not arbitrary — it is 1 − 1/e, the fraction any exponential response covers in exactly one τ.

Picture a thin metal spoon and a heavy iron pot over the same flame. The spoon heats in seconds, the pot in minutes. The whole difference is C and UA: more mass, or weaker cooling, means a longer τ — the part heats more slowly and cools more slowly too.

Now the numbers as validate.py prints them. All three come from a step response inside the model — hold the engine steady, change the condition abruptly, and watch how fast the temperature settles — and not one of them from a measurement on the car. Turbine housing: 48.0 seconds, at 3000 rpm, 140 kPa and 108 kW, against a published band of 40–120. Oil: 16.0 seconds, which falls outside the published band of 20–400, and we report it as it stands. Coolant: only 9.5 seconds.

Do not read that 9.5 as "a small mass". It is the apparent time constant of a regulated system: what holds the temperature there is the thermostat, not the metal. The engine mass really is slow — before the thermostat opens, C/UA gives 105 000 J/K divided by 45 W/K, about 39 minutes. Once the thermostat opens and the fan runs, UA rises and the response falls to tens of seconds. For the turbine housing, the direct C/UA calculation gives 50.3 seconds against 48.0 from the step response: two different routes to the same answer, which is a check rather than a coincidence.

The project's second system to control — a battery pack — has no number in the repository at all. That work has not started and battery.py does not exist, so any range we show for the battery is illustrative only, and we have to say so in the same breath.

And here is the heart of it. H is the preview horizon: how many seconds ahead we can see what the road is about to do. The simulator gives the agent the slope of the road 2, 5, 15 and 30 seconds ahead, and the H/τ sweep — running the same experiment over and over, changing one quantity across a range and holding everything else fixed — keeps H at 30 seconds throughout.

H/τ compares our horizon with the slowness of the part we are protecting. It is a pure number, with no units. If the part is far slower than our horizon — τ much greater than H — then 30 seconds of warning tells us nothing we could act on in time; the problem takes minutes to build. If the part is far faster — τ much less than H — preview is wasted, because the part responds the moment we ask it to, and simply reacting is enough. All the value lies in between, and where exactly it lies is what this project measures; we have not found it mapped in the literature.

Finally, the tempo, so you can picture what is actually happening: the agent issues a new command every 0.2 seconds, and one training run — an episode, start to finish — lasts 900 seconds. That is 4500 decisions in a single episode. And all of this physics lives inside a simulator. We matched that simulator to the car at 22 operating points — steady moments in the logged drives — and only between 30 and 74 kPa. Above that we lean on the published literature. That is what you say in the viva when you are asked how you know the simulator is right.

The time constant τ — the central quantity of the whole project

Change the part, and change how far ahead you can see
Preview horizon H 30 s
Time constant τ
Ratio H / τ

Map data gives you thirty seconds at most. The turbine housing has τ ≈ 48 s, so the engine alone can only reach H/τ ≈ 0.6 and there is no way to climb above it. A battery pack at τ ≈ 19 min opens the range from 0.03 to 3.20. That is why there is a second plant.

The three things the car lied to us about — treat every channel name as a hypothesis, not a fact

The channel as it names itselfWhat it actually isThe evidence that exposed it
Intake manifold absolute pressureA pre-throttle sensor, not one inside the manifoldAt idle the throttle is nearly shut, so the pressure inside the manifold should fall to about a third of atmospheric. This channel does not: warm, it reads 13.49 psi against an atmospheric 14.23 — barely any drop at all, which is how we know it is not measuring inside the manifold. Feed it into the model and the air-mass error is 75 %; work the pressure out from air flow instead and the error is 1.3 %.
Air mass flowA sensor that saturates at its ceiling and then goes on reporting as if nothing had happenedIt stops at exactly 1020.0 kg/h, on 517 samples across five different drives. A number that identical, repeating like that, does not happen in nature — the sensor has hit its ceiling and gone on reporting. Samples stuck at the ceiling are "pinned": we flag them maf_pinned and leave them out of the steady points we compare the model against.
Intake air temperature before throttle valveThe compressor outlet, not the temperature of the charge entering the cylinderIt reads 149 °C under boost and 163 °C at peak, and no intercooler — the radiator that cools compressed air before it reaches the engine — delivers air that hot. The decisive part is numerical, and the two sides are matched by construction rather than by choice: the logged side keeps every reading where the car's own Boost pressure channel is above 15 psi gauge, and (15 + 14.23) × 6.894757 = 201.5 kPa absolute, so gating the model side at 200 kPa selects the same operating region. At that matched gate — 587 boosted, MAF-unpinned model samples against 887 logged readings whose median is 226 kPa — this sensor puts the computed pressure at 279.5 kPa, +23.7 % above the car; the charge temperature our own model computes puts it at 232.7 kPa, +3.0 %. The reason is that the B58's cooler sits inside the intake manifold — after the throttle, not before it.

Why all of this matters — the five levers are exactly this physics

First, what "trim" means
The first three levers are trims. They do not command the engine directly; they are added to whatever the baseline has already commanded. The baseline is the conventional control program we simulate in engine_env.BaselineECU: a spark map built from 11 measured points between 43 and 79 kPa, knock-limited above that, plus the fourth version of our enrichment model, fitted on nine drives. A trim of zero means "do not intervene". The last two levers are absolute values: what the agent writes is the final command itself
spark trim
−8 … +4 crank degrees, added to the baseline's spark
Advance buys torque; retard escapes knock. And the more you retard, the hotter the exhaust — so this is a thermal lever and a torque lever at once
lambda trim
−0.15 … +0.06
The fastest thermal lever. Enrichment cools the turbo within moments, and the price is wasted fuel
boost/MAP trim
−40 … +15 kPa
Limits the air coming in, so torque and heat fall together. The costliest lever, because it is the only one that openly refuses the torque the driver asked for — and delivering that torque is a hard constraint in the reward, not a preference
cooling fan duty
0 … 1
An absolute duty — the fraction of full output, 0 for off and 1 for full speed — and what the agent writes is the final command, not an addition to one. Running the fan raises UA, which shortens the time constant: the slow mass cools faster
coolant pump duty
0.3 … 1
An absolute duty as well, with a floor of 0.3 because a coolant pump never stops completely
In short
The five levers are not abstract numbers. Each one puts an idea from this page into action: timing, mixture, air, cooling. And the agent is not learning "to drive". It is learning one thing — when to pay a small price now, a little torque or a little fuel, to avoid heat it cannot take back thirty seconds later
Quick glossary (come back to it whenever you forget)
agent the reinforcement-learning program we train to set the levers · baseline the conventional control program we compare it against · trim an adjustment added to the baseline's command · duty the fraction of full output, 0 to 1 · TDC top dead centre · BTDC crank degrees before it · ATDC after it · MBT the earliest timing that still gives maximum torque · knock self-ignition of the unburnt mixture, the destructive one · KI the knock integral, and knock at 1 · load how much air enters the cylinder on one stroke · boost pressure above atmospheric · λ actual air over stoichiometric air · MAP manifold absolute pressure · EGT exhaust gas temperature · C thermal mass · UA heat leaking out per degree of difference · τ = C/UA the slowness of a part · H the preview horizon, 30 seconds · H/τ the ratio the project rests on · sweep running the model again and again, changing one quantity across a range and holding everything else fixed · channel one named signal the car publishes through its diagnostic port · operating point one steady moment in a logged drive, used to compare model against car · ECU the engine control unit, which we read from and never write to
Say this if a supervisor stops you in the corridorAn engine turns small repeated explosions into rotation. What limits how hard you can push it is not the twisting force it makes — it is knock (destructive self-ignition) and heat. We protect one component: the turbine housing. Its time constant in our model is 48 seconds, and that is the denominator of the H/τ ratio the whole project rests on.
03
Prior art

Prior Work

You can already buy the original idea. So what is left?

The original idea — reading the road ahead and getting the engine and gearbox ready before the load arrives — is a commercial product you can buy today, built on map and GPS data rather than machine learning. Nobody has measured what preview is worth.

13.4 percentage points Thermal damage cut: 47.2 % with preview against 33.8 % without check_premise.py — hand-written policies inside the simulation, at the 1123 K turbine protection threshold. No reinforcement learning agent has been trained yet.
16.5 to 26.0 percentage points Preview advantage widens as H/τ falls 4.47 → 0.60 generality_test.py — the H2 table, at the 1123 K turbine protection threshold. At a 930 K threshold the figures were 0.0, 0.1 and 0.2 — the engine did not change, only the threshold did.
548.6 = 548.6 Preview disabled collapses exactly onto reactive check_premise.py — rows two and four, at the 1123 K threshold
5 works Closest works in the threat table, ranked from most threatening to least DOC/Novelty_Statement.pdf section 2 for the five in the threat table; DOC/Slide_By_Slide_Team_Brief.pdf slide 4 for the sixth (Bedei et al.)

The uncomfortable truth, first

The original idea already exists, and you can buy it today. Mercedes-Benz Trucks sells Predictive Powertrain Control: it reads map and GPS data, sees the climb before the truck reaches it, and chooses the gear and the cruising speed before it starts.

That is what preview means: the system knows what is coming in the next few seconds of driving — or, on a map, the next few kilometres, which the vehicle's speed turns back into seconds.

Note the distinction, because it will keep coming back. Prediction is a guess the model computes from the state of the system now. Preview is real information about the future that comes from outside the system — a map, a radar return, a route known in advance.

The first guesses. The second knows. Our question is about the second kind: what is that knowledge worth?

If we told the examining committee we were building a predictive engine controller, the right answer would be: that already exists, and it ships on trucks today. There is no defending that, so we should say it before they do.

So we changed the question. We are not building a predictive controller — we are measuring what preview is worth, which is the question that comes before building any controller. What we want out of it is a rule an engineer can apply in advance: preview is worth buying once the horizon you can see passes some fraction of the time the protected part takes to heat up. Nobody has put a number on that fraction. This section is about who has come closest.

Some background, before the table

The system we are protecting is a turbocharged engine. A turbocharger is two fans on one shaft: one driven by the hot exhaust leaving the engine, the other compressing the air going into it. The hot side is the turbine, and the longer it runs hot, the shorter its life.

On the simulator we measured that this turbine takes about 48 seconds to make most of its climb to a new temperature after the load rises — roughly two thirds of the way there. That settling time is the time constant, written τ and said "tau". If the map shows us the hill 30 seconds before we reach it, then H = 30 s, τ = 48 s, and H/τ ≈ 0.6.

The criterion is only worth anything if it works on more than one machine, so the plan is to test it on a second one. Control engineers call the thing being controlled the plant; our second plant is a battery, chosen because it heats up by completely different physics from an engine. What we would protect there is the temperature of the battery pack, exactly as we protect the turbine here. If the two systems fall on the same curve when we plot them against H/τ, the criterion holds. If they do not, the criterion is wrong — we say so, and that too is a result.

The battery does not exist yet: there is no battery.py in the repository, and our own claim sheet marks the two-plant test, in that one word, "Untested". We say it now, before anyone asks.

Next comes a glossary, then a table of the five closest works. Do not skip to the table. The glossary before it defines every unfamiliar word in about two minutes, and the table is far easier to read afterwards.

The column headed "threat to our claim" asks one question: how close does this work already come to what we say we are doing first? The rows are ordered by threat, not by fame.

Five terms, read these first — two minutes

The ratio H/τ — the heart of the whole projectH over tau

H is the preview horizon: how many seconds ahead we can see the road. τ (said "tau") is the time constant: how many seconds the component we are protecting takes to do most of its heating. After one time constant it is about two thirds of the way to its new temperature. Our entire claim is that what matters is neither H on its own nor τ on its own, but the ratio between them.

Why it matters here · If you can see 30 seconds ahead (H = 30) and the component takes 48 seconds to heat (τ = 48), you watch a good part of the problem form before it hurts you, and preview earns its keep. If the component heats up in two seconds, your 30-second view buys you nothing: the event is over before you can act. And if it needs ten minutes, you are looking at a problem that will not arrive before the drive ends.

H∞ control — designing for the worst caseH-infinity

A disturbance is anything that pushes the system off course: a hill that starts without warning, a trailer that adds load, air coming in hotter than the design assumed. H∞ — say it "H-infinity" — is a design method that minimises the worst the disturbance can do. Not the worst case you expect: the worst case there is, whatever shape it takes and however fast it arrives. It does not promise a good average. It promises a ceiling: whatever hits you, the error will not exceed this bound.

Why it matters here · Like sizing an air conditioner for the hottest day in August, not for the average of the year.

RegretRegret

Regret is the gap between how you performed and how an ideal controller would have performed if it had known the whole future in advance. The smaller the regret, the closer you are to ideal.

Why it matters here · Picture one exam. You sat it knowing only what you had revised; a classmate had seen the paper the day before. The gap between your two marks is the regret. One recent paper — the February 2026 preprint that heads the table below — proves that regret converges toward zero as preview grows. That means: the further ahead you can see, the closer your mark gets to the mark of someone who saw the whole paper. It proves that this happens, but not how fast. That missing rate is what we measure.

Spectral radiusSpectral radius

A single number that sums up how slow a system is: how long the effect of one push lingers before it fades. A value closer to one means a system that forgets slowly, carrying the trace of what happened to it for a long time. It measures the same thing as our time constant τ, but in discrete time — that is, for a system computed step by step rather than followed as a continuous flow.

Why it matters here · This is why we call the preprint's result a linear-systems echo of our H/τ claim: it ties the value of preview to how slow the system is, exactly as we do — but in a much simpler world, and without ever putting a number on it.

Open-loop and closed-loopOpen-loop / Closed-loop

Open-loop is the system on its own, with no controller correcting it: push it, let go, and watch how long it takes to settle by itself. Closed-loop is the system and its controller together: it measures the error moment by moment and corrects it.

Why it matters here · This is why the slowness of a system gets measured twice: once on its own, and once under control. The preprint talks about closed-loop slowness; the Model Predictive Control (MPC) rule of thumb in the table below talks about open-loop settling time — how long the system takes to settle with no controller helping it.

The five closest works, from the most threatening to our claim to the least

Prior workWhat it establishesThreat to our claimWhy it does not close the gap — this is the column that matters
Regret of H∞ Preview Controllers arXiv preprint 2602.01420, February 2026 (An arXiv preprint is a paper posted publicly before independent reviewers have seen it. This one is very recent and not yet reviewed, so we cite it as a preprint, and we will not name its authors until we have checked the arXiv listing itself.)It establishes that the benefit of preview has a ceiling. The longer you make the horizon, the less each extra second adds, until the gain almost stops growing. It also shows that how quickly you reach that ceiling depends on how slow the system is: the slower the system, the longer the horizon that still pays. This is the closest published result to ours.HighestIts result holds only for linear time-invariant systems (LTI): simplified systems where doubling the input doubles the output, and whose character does not change over time. Our engine is not one of them. It is theory on paper, illustrated on a single numerical example, not measured across a range of cases. Most of all: it says the benefit stops growing, but not when or by how much. It never yields a usable rule — nothing of the form "preview is worth buying once the horizon passes this fraction of the time constant". That rule is exactly what we are building.
The common rule of thumb for choosing the prediction horizon in Model Predictive Control (MPC)A rule passed round among engineers: make the horizon long enough to cover the time the system takes to settle by itself after an upset.HighThis is advice for tuning a controller you have already decided to build, not for deciding whether information about the future is worth its price. It does not say how much performance you gain, nor when you gain nothing. It is a rule of thumb handed down by experience; nobody has ever measured it and put a number on it. Someone will say: just run MPC, measure it, and be done. That is exactly what our planned MPC oracle baseline will do, and it is the right way to measure one system. But it demands a validated model, a chosen horizon, and a solver that works before it tells you anything. The criterion answers first, from two numbers an engineer can estimate on the back of an envelope. MPC is how we measure what preview is worth on one system; the criterion is what we distil from many such measurements, so that the next engineer does not have to make them.
Mercedes-Benz Predictive Powertrain Control A commercial product you can buy todayMap-based preview driving the cruise control and the gear selection of heavy trucks, to save fuel.MediumIt establishes that predictive control works and pays commercially — but for one system, one objective, and one horizon. There is no published account of how the benefit scales with the length of the horizon; the company sells the result and does not publish the curve. It acts on road speed and gear choice. We act on something else: keeping a part inside the engine from running too hot for too long, because that heat is what wears it out.
Reinforcement learning for engine control ORNL (Maldonado et al. 2024), NREL (Henry de Frahan et al. 2022), and a 2026 surveyReinforcement learning applied to two engine problems: dilute combustion control (running the engine on more air and less fuel) and injection timing (when in the cycle the fuel goes in). The third item is a survey of machine-learning methods in engine calibration.MediumThese show that reinforcement learning already works on engine control problems, which supports the feasibility of our project rather than threatening its claim. We cite them; we do not fear them. But nothing in them asks: what is information about the future worth? Nor does any of it try the same rule on two systems that differ in their physics — an engine that burns and a battery that charges — and that is exactly our test.
Predictive energy management in hybrid and electric vehiclesA wide literature on energy management when the route is known in advance, on vehicle speed prediction, and on eco-driving.LowEvery paper in it improves one system with one kind of preview. This field has never asked the question that sits above all of them: what governs the value of preview when you move from one system to another? That is our only question.

A sixth paper we should name outright

  • Bedei et al. — constrained reinforcement learning, tested on a real engine — The closest work to us in method: constrained reinforcement learning on a real engine. (The preprint above is the closest in question; this one is the closest in technique.) "Constrained" means the agent — the learning controller — is given limits it may not break however tempting the reward: a temperature ceiling that must not be crossed, for instance. It was validated on real engine hardware, not in simulation alone. We still owe this one a full citation: we have the authors and the finding, not yet the year and the venue.
  • Why naming it helps us — Because it has no preview at all, and the authors say so in the paper itself. So it gives us something we need — evidence that this method works on a real engine and not only in simulation — and leaves our question exactly as open as it was.

What makes our numbers a measurement rather than an impression

  • The blind test: 548.6 against 548.6 — Damage here is an index, not a physical quantity: the model adds up how far and for how long the turbine sits above the temperature at which it starts to age. Only the ratios between runs mean anything; the absolute number does not. We take the same predictive policy — the rule the controller follows — and switch off its information about the future, changing nothing else. It collapses exactly onto the reactive policy, the rule that acts only once the temperature has already risen: 548.6 against 548.6, to the decimal. That proves the whole gap comes from preview, not from a smarter agent and not from better tuning.
  • The threshold everything was measured at: 1123 K — This is the turbine temperature at which damage starts to accelerate, and we chose it from the damage model itself rather than from the statistics of any one simulated run. It is not a detail. The same sweep of preview horizons — the one behind the 16.5-to-26.0 figure below — gave advantages of 0.0, 0.1 and 0.2 percentage points when the threshold was set at 930 K instead. The engine did not change; only the threshold did. So no preview-advantage figure is quoted without the threshold it was measured against.
  • Preview is not free: the fuel goes up — In the same scenario: 4091 grams of fuel for the baseline — the stock controller, with no protection added — 4175 for reactive protection, and 4314 for predictive. check_premise.py prints all three in the same table as the damage figures. We are buying the reduction in damage with more fuel. The trade-off is stated, not hidden.
  • Where the numbers stop — The preview advantage widens from 16.5 to 26.0 percentage points as H/τ falls from 4.47 to 0.60. At ratios lower still, the component heats so slowly that it never reaches the temperature limit at all, and there is nothing left to measure. We say that rather than extending the line into a region where we have no data.
  • No agent has been trained yet — All of these numbers come from hand-written policies inside the simulator, not from a reinforcement learning agent — training is the next phase. Saying it ourselves protects us in the viva. If the examiner finds it instead, it stops being a limitation and becomes the only thing the viva is about.

The honest summary of the state of the field

There is very mature work on how to build a preview controller: how to design it, how to tune it, how to ship it in a truck.

There is one theoretical result saying that the benefit of preview saturates — that is, it reaches a ceiling and stops growing however long you make the horizon. But it says this for simplified linear systems only.

What nobody has done is measure what preview is worth on a real system. Real here means three things at once: nonlinear, so doubling the input does not double the output; constrained hard, with a temperature that must not be exceeded rather than a target it would be nice to hit; and answerable before anything is built, in a form an engineer can actually use.

That gap is where this project sits. Theory says the benefit exists and has a ceiling; we say how large it is, and when it is worth paying for.

What does that mean in practice for an engineer? It means deciding before paying: is the map, or the radar, or the data link worth its price on this particular system? Today preview is bought first and its effect discovered afterwards. We want the engineer to be able to work out the answer from two numbers before signing for it.

Why we name the closest paper ourselves

  • The examiner will reach this paper within a minute — Anyone who knows preview control will remember the February 2026 preprint immediately. If we do not name it, they will — and the difference between those two cases is the difference between a student who has read the literature and one who has not.
  • Naming is stronger than denial — Naming the closest work and saying precisely what it leaves open is worth far more than claiming there is no prior work. The second claim can be demolished with one search.
  • But do not attribute it to authors before checking — The Novelty Statement gives only the identifier and the date: arXiv 2602.01420, February 2026. The team brief already attributes it to Liu and Seiler — that attribution is unverified, and it is exactly the one to check. Before any author's name goes into Chapter 2, we open the arXiv listing and confirm it. Getting the authorship of "the closest work to us" wrong costs more than leaving the names out.
  • The sentence that earns trust — "The closest work to us is a preprint published in February 2026 on regret bounds in preview control. It establishes that the benefit of preview reaches a ceiling in simplified linear systems, but it does not say how large the benefit is, nor at what horizon it stops growing. That is exactly what we measure, and on a nonlinear system rather than a linear one."

A caution: this is a targeted search, not a systematic review

  • What it covers — Preview control theory, predictive powertrain control, and reinforcement learning for engine calibration. We chose these three fields because they are the likeliest to be hiding work that contradicts our claim. (A full systematic review is a piece of research in its own right, and we have not done one — and we say so.)
  • What it does not cover — Industrial process control, robotics, building climate control (HVAC), and electrical power systems. Any one of them could hold the same criterion under a completely different name.
  • What is needed: one day from one of us — A search in those fields under the terms: value of information, preview gain, anticipatory control, lookahead benefit.
  • If something contradicting us turns up — Finding it in the second week is a thousand times better than finding it in the viva. Its existence does not kill the project; it turns it into an independent replication and an extension, and both of those are legitimate results.
  • Say this to the supervisor plainly — A student who states the limits of their search looks careful. A student who claims to have read everything looks naive, and attracts exactly the scrutiny they were trying to avoid.
Say this if a supervisor stops you in the corridorTheory says the benefit of preview has a ceiling, but not how high. Industry sells preview today without ever measuring when it is worth the price, and the closest reinforcement-learning work has no preview in it at all. What is missing is the measurement, and that is what we provide.
04
Our contribution

A criterion, not a controller

We are not building a controller — we are measuring when preview is worth buying

Imagine the car knows, half a minute ahead, that a long climb is coming. That is preview, and it has a price. We do not build the controller that uses it — we measure when it is worth buying: how many seconds you can see ahead, divided by how many seconds the part we protect takes to heat up.

13.4 percentage points preview advantage in the premise check check_premise.py — damage column, with the environment's damage model and a 1123 K protection limit (≈ 850 °C)
26.0 percentage points preview advantage in the H/τ sweep — a different experiment generality_test.py — H2 table at the same 1123 K limit, but with a different scenario (520 s at dt 2.0 against the premise check's 720 s at dt 1.0) and a different damage cost (turbine-only exponential, re-scored, rather than the environment's three-term damage), which is why it is not 13.4
0.60 H/τ the engine's ceiling on its own generality_test.py — H2 table (turbine constant ≈ 50 s, horizon fixed at 30 s)
3.20 H/τ the most the battery is expected to reach (not measured) two-plant roadmap document §3 — a planned horizon table, not a measurement (the battery plant has not been built yet)

The pivot: we changed the question, not the tools

The original idea — "an AI that sees a heavy demand coming and adjusts the powertrain before it arrives" — is sold today as a commercial product. Mercedes sells it on trucks, and reinforcement learning in engine control is a published, active field. If we described the project that way, an examiner would be right to say it already exists.

So we changed the question. We are not building a predictive controller; we are measuring when preview information is worth buying in the first place.

Who benefits? An engineer facing a real purchasing decision: do I pay for map data, for an extra sensor, or for a mobile data connection to the vehicle? Today that question is settled by intuition, or by buying each option and trying it. We hand that engineer a number they can compute before they pay.

Our literature search was bounded. It covered preview control theory, predictive powertrain control, and reinforcement learning in engine calibration. It did not cover process control, robotics, building HVAC or power systems. Within those limits, we found no published work that gives a dimensionless criterion for how much preview is worth and then measures it, rather than proving it on paper.

The systems we have in mind are hard ones. They are nonlinear: doubling the input does not double the output. They carry hard constraints: limits you may not cross even for a few seconds, such as a temperature above which the metal itself begins to degrade.

And damage grows faster than the overshoot that causes it: the last fifty degrees of a temperature rise do several times the damage of the first two hundred. So a short, severe overshoot costs more than a long, mild one — which means a good controller should spend its effort shaving the peaks rather than holding a low average.

No criterion of this kind has been tested on more than one plant.

Read this before any number on the page

  • Two numbers from two experiments, not two numbers for one result — The 13.4-point figure comes from the premise check — our first experiment, described below — on a single scenario, meaning one simulated drive. The 26.0-point figure comes from the time-constant sweep. These are two different experiments: a different scenario and a different way of scoring damage, though the policies themselves are the same code. Always say which experiment produced the number, and which protection limit it was measured against.
  • Everything that follows happens inside a simulation — Both plants are models on a computer, not hardware in a lab. The engine simulation is written from physical equations and calibrated against 175.5 minutes of logs from a real car, but it is still a model.
  • We never write anything to the car's ECU — the engine control unit, the computer that runs the engine — We only read from it, through the car's standard diagnostic socket (OBD-II): we never reprogram it, never send it a command, and never change its calibration. Every control experiment runs inside the simulator. This is a hard constraint on the project, with no exceptions.
  • Where we actually are, phase by phase — The project runs in seven phases, A to G. Setup (A) is done. Matching the simulator to the car (B) is done. Training the agent (C) is next, and nothing has been trained yet. Baselines and the ablation (D), the battery plant (E) and the writing (G) have not started. The H/τ sweep (F) — the ratio is explained below — has a preliminary result from hand-written policies.

Our claim is: an experimentally validated, dimensionless criterion for the value of preview, on nonlinear systems with hard constraints, across physically different plants. Every word in it is load-bearing — delete any one and somebody else's published work already covers us.

Experimentally validated
The closest work to ours is a theory paper from February 2026: Liu & Seiler, "Regret of H∞ Preview Controllers", arXiv 2602.01420. It proves that the value of preview stops growing beyond a certain horizon — past some point, seeing further adds nothing. But it does not say where that point lies, or how much preview saves before it, and it treats linear systems with a proof rather than a measurement. We give measured magnitudes on a simulated engine built from physical equations and calibrated against 175.5 minutes of logs from a real car.
Delete this word and that paper already covers our claim. Raise it yourself in the viva — anyone who knows the field will think of it within a minute.
Dimensionless criterion
A result written in seconds and kelvin does not travel between domains. A result written in H/τ does. Travelling between domains is the whole point.
Delete it and this becomes a result about one engine.
Nonlinear, with hard constraints
Every preview theory we found is built on linear time-invariant systems: the response doubles when the input doubles, and the system's properties do not change over time. The engine is not like that. And it has two limits that must not be crossed. The first is knock: fuel igniting on its own before the spark is due, which can break an engine within seconds. The second is the temperature limit of the turbine housing — the iron casing described below, which the exhaust heats. Cross either one and the cost rises faster than the amount by which you crossed it. This is exactly where linear theory stops giving answers.
Delete it and you walk into the region existing linear theory already covers.
Across physically different plants
A result from one system is an anecdote, not a criterion. Combustion and electrochemistry share almost none of their physics, so a curve that fits both is evidence of something general.
Delete it and the claim is about an engine, not about a principle.

H/τ from scratch

H is the preview horizon: how many seconds you can see ahead before the load arrives. Load here means how hard the engine is being asked to work — the torque the driver is demanding, and with it the heat the engine has to get rid of. A long uphill climb is a high, sustained load.

The part we protect is the turbine housing. It is an iron casing that carries the engine's hot exhaust gas onto a small turbine wheel; that wheel drives a compressor, which forces extra air into the engine. In our climb scenario it reaches 879 °C, and above about 850 °C — the 1123 K protection limit quoted with every result on this page — damage starts to accumulate quickly. You cannot cool it at the push of a button, because it is a heavy piece of iron: slow to heat, slow to cool.

τ is the time constant: the measure of that slowness. On our engine it is around 50 seconds, which means that after a sudden change in load it takes about 50 seconds to cover two thirds of the way to its new temperature.

Picture two things you cook on. First, a large pot on a low flame: if someone warns you half a minute before it boils, the warning buys you nothing — the pot takes minutes to get there, and you would see it coming anyway.

Second, a very thin frying pan: it heats and cools almost instantly, so simply lifting it off the flame brings it straight back down. Here too the warning adds nothing, because the cure is faster than the warning.

Advance warning only pays in the middle: when the part you are protecting is slow enough to be damaged before you can catch it, and fast enough that thirty seconds of warning changes what you can do about it. The engine sits in that middle.

Put precisely: when τ is much larger than H, you are seeing thirty seconds into a problem that takes ten minutes to develop. When τ is much smaller than H, the part settles to its new temperature long before the warning could have changed anything. The useful region is where the two numbers are comparable — and nobody has mapped it.

That is where our practical problem starts. On its own the engine only reaches H/τ ≈ 0.6: the turbine time constant is about 50 seconds, and navigation maps give us at most about 30 seconds of warning about the gradient ahead. Thirty divided by fifty is 0.6, and with one plant there is no way past that ceiling — which is why we need a second one.

The H/τ sweep — the fixed-limit H2 table

Same policies, same physics; the only variable is the turbine's heat capacity

Read the direction, not the numbers. The edge grows as the component gets heavier and slower — 16.5 → 18.0 → 26.0 — until it is so slow that it never gets hot enough to matter. That is exactly what the physical argument predicts.

Say this with every figure

These numbers were 0.0 / 0.1 / 0.2 at the old 930 K protection limit. Nothing about the plant changed — only the threshold did. A preview advantage quoted without the limit it was measured against is not a result.

We expected preview value to rise as the cost curve became more convex — the intuition being that a sharp cost rewards spreading the load over time. The same three trajectories, re-scored under different cost curvatures:

Cost modelreactivepredictiveedge
linear p = 161.8 %98.6 %36.8
p = 1.577.2 %99.7 %22.5
quadratic p = 286.5 %99.9 %13.4
p = 395.4 %100.0 %4.6
p = 498.5 %100.0 %1.5
exponential73.2 %99.1 %26.0

It fell. From 36.8 points at linear cost to 1.5 at p = 4. The reason is obvious in hindsight: a more convex cost makes the reactive policy itself better, because the penalty becomes sharp enough to be worth backing off from even without foreknowledge. The hypothesis goes into the results chapter exactly as it came out.

Why "dimensionless", and why two plants

  • Dimensionless means portable — Dimensionless means the number carries no seconds, no metres, no degrees. A 16:9 screen is the example everyone knows: the same ratio on a phone and in a cinema, because the centimetres cancel in the division and what is left describes shape, not size. Engineers do this constantly — the Reynolds number puts flow inside a pipe and flow over an aircraft wing on one scale, for the same reason. H/τ is our version, and being dimensionless is exactly what lets a result from a petrol engine carry over to a battery pack.
  • One plant gives a point, not a curve — Two plants with overlapping but differently scaled ranges give you a curve, and with it a prediction you can check. The falsifiable claim: points from both plants fall on a single curve when plotted against H/τ.
  • Why this battery in particular, not any other slow system — A battery pack's time constant is about 19 minutes (1125 s), because its thermal mass is enormous. And how much of the route is known in advance varies from thirty seconds to a full hour. So the battery alone spans a factor of about a hundred in H/τ, from 0.03 to 3.20, and it is built from published equations with no hardware and no lab. The battery is not a second example; it is the measuring stick that makes the criterion testable.
  • And a warning: this is a plan, not a result — The battery plant has not been built yet (Phase E). The 19-minute figure is a design target computed from the pack specification, and the plan's own uncertainty range is 9 to 38 minutes. Our design document is explicit about the rule: τ must be measured from an open-loop step response, never inferred from the model's own coefficients — otherwise we could be accused of choosing τ to make the curves line up.

The planned pack, five values of H/τ (τ = 1125 s ≈ 18.75 min — a design target; the declared range is 9–38 min)

Preview availableHorizon HH/τWhat we expect to happen (expectation, not measurement)
Immediate road ahead30 s0.03Preview should be worthless
Next manoeuvre2 min0.11Marginal
Next segment of route10 min0.53Comparable to the engine
Remaining journey30 min1.60Beyond anything the engine reaches
Full planned route60 min3.20Should saturate — test this
WarningPhase E has not started—A design-specification table, not a measurement; τ will be measured from a step response before any point on the curve is produced

How we measure it — claim 1, already run in the simulator with hand-written policies

  1. Two controllers, with exactly the same hands
    A reactive controller and a predictive one, given the same actuators and exactly the same limits. The actuators are the five things they can actually move: spark timing, how much extra fuel is added to the mixture, boost pressure (how hard the turbocharger forces air into the engine), the cooling fan and the water pump. Neither controller has a button the other does not.
  2. The only difference: when they act
    The reactive controller waits until the turbine temperature it is measuring right now starts to rise. The predictive controller acts on the road gradient it expects 15 and 30 seconds ahead. In the code there is no other difference between them.
  3. Ablation — the idea from scratch
    Remove one component, re-run, measure the difference. Like taking the salt out of a recipe to find out what the salt was doing. Here we zero the preview channels — that is, we stop the predictive policy from seeing the road ahead — and re-run it without changing anything else.
  4. And what "damage" even is
    A number that accumulates every second from three sources: turbine housing temperature above its limit, oil temperature above its limit, and knock. Each of the three terms grows faster than the amount by which the limit is exceeded — the two temperature terms exponentially, knock quadratically — so a severe overshoot costs several times what a mild one does. It has no units, and what matters is the comparison, not the number on its own.
  5. The result, and it is the strongest fact in the project
    On exactly the same drive: the baseline (what the car's own ECU does) 829.2, reactive 548.6, predictive 437.6. Then we zero the preview channels and re-run the same predictive policy: 548.6 — landing exactly on the reactive number, to the decimal. All of these numbers come from the simulator (engine_env.py) on the standard scenario: a 12 % gradient — a steep, sustained climb — held at 110 km/h in 42 °C air, twelve minutes long: three flat, then nine climbing.
  6. What that means, and under what condition
    The reactive controller cuts damage by 33.8 %, the predictive one by 47.2 %. The gap is 13.4 percentage points — the difference between the two percentages, attributable to the preview information and to nothing else. And quote the limit with every figure: the protection limit is 1123 K (≈ 850 °C — metal that glows). A preview advantage without the limit it was measured against is not a result. We do not convert these percentage points into component life, or into a number of mountain climbs; that would need metal-fatigue data we do not have.
  7. And at what maturity — say it before you are asked
    Both policies behind these numbers are hand-written (if-then rules), not learned, and no reinforcement learning agent has been trained yet. That is deliberate: first prove the gap exists and can be measured by a clean procedure, then replace the hand-written policy with a trained agent. Training the agent is the next step.

Three claims, and how each one falls — a claim that cannot fall is not worth making

Claim 1 · a measurement method
A repeatable procedure for measuring the value of preview on any plant: two controllers identical in their actuators, differing only in timing, and preview channels that can be ablated.
High confidence — already run on the engine inside the simulator with hand-written policies: 548.6 against 548.6 once preview is zeroed.
Claim 2 · H/τ governs the magnitude
The value of preview is governed by one number: the preview horizon divided by the time constant of the state that the limit acts on. Here that state is the turbine housing temperature — the quantity we are keeping below its limit, and the slowest thing in the plant. Not the domain, not the kind of actuator, not the units.
This is the thesis, and its evidence is preliminary. The sweep does not use the real turbine. We hold the policies and the physics fixed and change only the housing's thermal mass, from 800 to 18 000 J/K, which moves τ from 6.7 s to 150.9 s and H/τ from 4.47 to 0.20. The real engine sits at 0.60, one point on that line; the rest of the line is what a heavier or lighter component would give. With only the time constant swept, the advantage rose 16.5 → 18.0 → 26.0 percentage points at the fixed 1123 K protection limit, as H/τ fell from 4.47 to 0.60. (At the old 930 K limit those were 0.0/0.1/0.2 — the limit is what changed, not the physics.) Watch the direction, because it is not obvious: the heavier the turbine, the larger τ, the smaller H/τ, and the bigger the advantage. And from H/τ = 0.20 downward the turbine becomes so heavy that it never reaches the limit at all: there is nothing to protect, and the two policies tie. So the benefit climbs and then collapses; it does not grow without bound — and that is the "useful region", measured rather than described. And say it in full. Phase F was designed around one protocol, which we call H2b: it sets the protection limit per configuration at the 80th percentile of the unprotected run. On this scenario that breaks — the climb spends most of its time at peak temperature, so the 80th percentile lands on the peak, and three of the five rows pin at 100 % for both policies, which means the two cannot be told apart. So for now we use H2, the table with a single fixed limit, and replacing the threshold rule is the project's declared open problem.
Claim 3 · it transfers between domains
Points from a combustion engine and from an electrochemical battery fall on a single curve when plotted against H/τ.
Not tested yet, and the most likely of the three to fall. That is why the second plant exists.
Why this ordering protects the project
If claim 3 falls, claims 1 and 2 still stand as a complete thesis, and we report the failure of claim 3 as a result in its own right, not as a collapse.
Order your claims so that the whole project does not fall with the weakest one.
And one of our hypotheses has already fallen — H1
We expected the value of preview to grow as the cost of damage rose more steeply with overshoot. We re-scored the same three trajectories under six cost functions, from linear to exponential. The result: under the exponential cost the advantage came out at 0.7 of its value under the linear one — it shrank instead of growing.
The hypothesis is rejected and we report it as it is. A declared negative result is stronger evidence of honesty than a polished positive one.
Refutation · if the two plants' curves do not overlap
Then H/τ is necessary but not sufficient. At that point we go looking for the second dimensionless number we are missing — most likely actuator authority against disturbance size: how much the controller can actually change, compared with the size of what hits the system.
We publish the criterion as incomplete and name the missing dimension. That is still a contribution.
Refutation · if the advantage does not vary with H/τ on the battery
Then the engine result was an accident of that plant's structure, not a law.
We report the negative result, and go back to whether the quantity we chose to protect is really the one that governs the plant.
Refutation · if somebody publishes it before us within the year
We cite it, reproduce its result on our two plants, and reframe the work as an independent replication with an extension to constrained nonlinear systems.
A replication is publishable and honest, and finding it in week two is better than finding it in the viva.

Vocabulary — the physical terms first, then the learning machinery

Plant

The thing we control and protect. We have two: a petrol engine, and an electric battery pack that has not been built yet. The word is a control-engineering term; it does not mean a factory, a power station, or anything growing in a pot.

Actuator

What the controller can actually move. On the engine there are five: spark timing, how much extra fuel is added to the mixture, boost pressure, the cooling fan and the water pump.

Preview horizon · H

How many seconds you see ahead before the load arrives — the load being how hard the engine is about to be asked to work. For us, about 30 seconds, taken from road-gradient data in navigation maps. That data is not available on every road, and paying for it is part of the cost we are measuring.

Time constant · τ

A measure of how slow the protected part is: after a sudden change, the time it takes to cover two thirds of the way to the new state. On our turbine, two estimates agree: 50.3 seconds from the housing's heat capacity divided by its heat-loss coefficient, and 48.0 seconds from the model's response to a sudden step change in load. Both sit inside the published band of 40 to 120 seconds. It has never been measured on the car itself — there is no turbine temperature sensor fitted.

Damage

A unitless number that accumulates every second from turbine temperature, oil temperature and knock. The number means nothing by itself. It is only ever used to compare one policy with another on the same drive.

Knock

Fuel igniting on its own before the spark is due. It makes a metallic knocking sound, and it will break an engine within seconds if it continues.

Baseline

What the car's own ECU does, modelled inside the simulator and calibrated against real logs. All of our results are relative to it. And a guessed baseline will flatter you — that is a mistake we made once and wrote down.

Policy

The rule that turns what the system sees now into an action. It can be if-then rules you wrote by hand, or a trained neural network.

Tier 0 — the physics model

A simulation of the engine written from physical equations and calibrated against logs from a real car. It is the closest thing we have to ground truth, but it is slow: training on it alone takes nights, not hours.

Tier 1 — the surrogate ensemble

A fast, learned approximation that imitates the slow simulation. An "ensemble" because it is several models rather than one: when they disagree, we know we have reached a region none of them learned well, and their answers there cannot be trusted. It exists only to make training fast enough.

Tier 2 — the supervisory agent

The agent that moves setpoints instead of driving the actuators directly. A setpoint is the target a conventional control system chases: "hold the temperature at this value". So our agent never commands the engine's actuators directly. It changes the targets, and the original safety system keeps running underneath it, blocking any dangerous command. This is the tier we write ourselves, and it is the measuring instrument, not the thesis — the thesis is the criterion.

The rule that keeps the work honest

Train on Tier 1 because it is fast, and score on Tier 0 because it is the reference.

Why it matters here · An agent scored inside the surrogate learns to exploit the surrogate's errors, not to protect the engine.

SAC — soft actor-critic

A reinforcement learning algorithm for continuous actions. An actor proposes the action, two independent critics estimate its value, and a reward for keeping its choices varied — an entropy bonus — stops the policy from locking onto one solution too early.

A constraint, not a weightConstrained MDP

"How many litres of petrol is one newton-metre of torque the driver asked for and did not get worth?" is a question with no defensible answer — but rolling the objectives into a single weighted sum forces you to invent one anyway. (MDP: Markov decision process, the standard mathematical frame for this kind of control problem.)

Why it matters here · The constraint says: the driver's demand is a requirement, not a preference. The optimisation then happens inside the space of ways to meet it.

Say this if a supervisor stops you in the corridorThe original idea is already a product you can buy today, so we changed the question: we measure the value of preview as a function of H/τ — the preview horizon over the time constant of the state the limit constrains — on two plants that share no physics. A plant here is the thing we control and protect: a petrol engine in the first case, a battery pack in the second.
05
What sets us apart

Difference From Prior Work

They build the controller that exploits the road ahead. We measure when that knowledge is worth its price — map, GPS, camera, data subscription, extra sensor — before you pay it.

The literature explains how to build a controller that exploits the road ahead. Our targeted search found nobody measuring what that knowledge is worth before building it. That is what we measure.

13.4 percentage points Preview advantage — 47.2 % damage cut against 33.8 %, at the 1123 K trigger check_premise.py — derived from the printed damage column: (829.2−548.6)/829.2 and (829.2−437.6)/829.2
548.6 damage index (dimensionless) Reactive and predictive-without-preview: the same number to the decimal check_premise.py — rows 2 and 4 of the printed damage column
26.0 percentage points Largest advantage in the sweep — H2 table at the fixed 1123 K limit, at H/τ = 0.60 generality_test.py — H2 table (as printed in README.md)
33 checks Document checks passing — last run 13 September 2026, with 230 figure mentions agreeing verify_docs.py

Before any number: eight definitions

Preview

The computer knows now what will happen thirty seconds from now, instead of waiting for it to happen.

Why it matters here · For us it is the slope of the road ahead at 2, 5, 15 and 30 seconds, read off a route known in advance. In our experiments so far the preview is perfectly accurate — an assumption we state rather than hide, and we have not yet tested what happens when it is blurred.

H — the horizon

How many seconds we see ahead. Ours is 30 seconds, and it is fixed in every row of the sweep.

Why it matters here · Every H/τ ratio in this section has 30 seconds on top. Nothing else.

τ — the time constant

How many seconds the protected part needs to cover about two thirds of the way to its new temperature.

Why it matters here · A large pot of water on the hob: large τ. A cup of tea: small τ. Our turbine is about 50 seconds — 50.3 seconds from the calculation, and 48.0 seconds from a measured step response.

H/τ — the ratio

We see thirty seconds ahead of a part that needs about fifty seconds to heat up, so the ratio is 0.6.

Why it matters here · A number with no units. That is why it can be carried to any other system — a battery, or something else — and it is the whole bet of the project.

The turbine

A fan spun by hot exhaust gas, which compresses the engine's air. The hottest part in there, and the first to fail.

Why it matters here · We chose it for two reasons: its temperature is the limit that actually binds in our scenario, and its τ is close to our horizon. That closeness is what puts the project in the interesting region in the first place.

Damage

A cumulative dimensionless index, summed every second from three terms: turbine temperature above 1123 K, exponentially; oil temperature; and knock.

Why it matters here · Why it matters: the turbine is an expensive part to replace, and the car stops halfway up the Taif road when it is neglected. The number on its own means nothing; the meaning is in its ratio between two policies on the same drive.

Policy

Here this is not a learned agent. Every "policy" in this section is a rule we wrote by hand: if a condition holds, back off.

Why it matters here · Watch this one in particular, since you are coming from reinforcement learning. The agent has not been trained yet — that is Phase C.

The cost of preview

Preview is not free: a slope map, GPS, a camera, a data subscription, an extra sensor, and compute the car has to carry.

Why it matters here · And the cost repeats on every car sold, not once. The whole question of the project: is it worth that price, and how do you know before you pay it.

Them / us — one line for each prior work

Theory · limits on the value of preview (Liu & Seiler, regret bounds in H∞ control — arXiv preprint 2602.01420, February 2026)
Them: a mathematical proof that the benefit of preview has a ceiling it stops at. But in linear systems — double the input, double the output. They prove the benefit exists, not what it is worth, and not on any real hardware.
Us: measured numbers on one nonlinear system, and one with hard constraints — the turbine temperature ceiling is not a preference, it is a red line. And a second system planned but not yet built.
Industry · Mercedes Predictive Powertrain Control
Them: a commercial product on trucks. The truck knows from the map that a descent is coming, so it picks its speed and gear in advance and saves fuel. One system, one objective, one horizon.
Us: our objective is not fuel but keeping a part from burning — turbine temperature and turbine life. And our question: is preview worth it on something that is not a truck, and how do you know the answer before you build.
Reinforcement learning on engines · Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL) and the National Renewable Energy Laboratory (NREL) in the United States, and a 2026 survey
Them: they show that reinforcement learning genuinely works on real engines.
Us: that is on our side, not against us — it means the method is not a gamble. And none of them asked what preview itself is worth. Carrying a single criterion across two domains is what we are attempting, and it is not finished.
Model Predictive Control (MPC)
Them: MPC runs a model of the system at every instant, tries the upcoming decisions on it, then executes the best one. It is the measuring instrument, not the thing measured.
Us: we plan it as a reference baseline in Phase D, and it is not built — there is no MPC code in the repository today. Running it needs a validated model, a horizon you choose yourself, and a solver: the whole investment before you know whether it is worth it. Our criterion answers earlier, from two numbers on a whiteboard: H and τ.

The strongest single fact in the project

  • 548.6 against 548.6 Switching preview off drops the predictive policy exactly onto the reactive one — to the decimal.
  • The size of the effect has changed four times — 30 points → 4 points → 0.3 points → 13.4 points at the 1123 K limit Across two engines, two scenarios and two protection triggers. The 0.3 is the measurement on the correct engine at the old 930 K limit, where both policies saturated. Every one of those measurements was correct for its own system and its own limit.
  • The ablation held all four times We remove preview alone: same policy, same weights, same scenario, same random seed. The only change is that the road ahead becomes zeros. So whatever the size of the gap, its source is the advance information and nothing else.
  • The number is not the result. The ablation is the result. That is the sentence to defend in the viva.

The race · a 12 % grade held for twelve minutes in 42 °C air

Real traces from check_premise.py
Minute 12.0
0 °C 0 damage
0 °C 0 damage
0 °C 0 damage

The first three minutes are flat, then the climb starts. All three policies hold exactly the same five levers with the same limits; the only difference is when they act. The protection limit of 1123 K is not a matter of taste — it is the knee of the damage term exp((T−1123)/45), a statement about the component rather than about this episode.

The premise result — four policies on the same drive: three minutes of flat ground, then twelve minutes climbing a 12 % grade at 110 km/h in 42 °C air

PolicyFuel gDamage (dimensionless index)Peak turbine °CPeak oil °C
The car's own engine computer (ECU) — no intervention from us4091829.2879128
Reactive protection — acts after the temperature crosses the limit4175548.6859123
Predictive protection — acts before the climb, because it sees it coming4314437.6852109
The same predictive policy, with preview switched off4175548.6859123

How to read the table in ten seconds

Measured against 829.2: the reactive policy cuts damage by 33.8 %, the predictive one by 47.2 %. The difference is 13.4 percentage points, and that is the preview advantage.

All four policies are rules we wrote by hand on the measured temperature, not a learned agent: "if the temperature crosses the limit, back off" against "if the climb arrives in 30 seconds, back off now". Training the agent is Phase C and has not started. We chose rules deliberately: what the table measures is the value of the information, not the intelligence of the algorithm.

The predictive policy drinks more fuel: 4314 grams against 4091. That is the trade-off and there is nothing wrong with it — we paid 5.5 % in fuel and bought 47 % of the damage.

And notice that peak turbine temperature stays above 850 °C in every row. Damage is not set by the peak alone, but by how far the limit is exceeded and for how long, and it grows exponentially. All of this is at the 1123 K (850 °C) limit — never quote a preview advantage without the limit it was measured against.

The H/τ sweep — we make the turbine heavier in simulation four times, everything else held fixed (a preliminary result from hand-written policies, not a trained agent)

Turbine heat capacity J/K — how much heat it swallows before warmingτ time constant (seconds)H/τ — and we see 30 secondsDamage reduction — reactiveDamage reduction — predictivePreview advantage (percentage points)
8006.74.4779.6 %96.1 %16.5
250021.01.4378.7 %96.7 %18.0
600050.30.6073.2 %99.1 %26.0
18000150.90.20——the temperature never reaches the limit — nothing to protect it from

The lesson buried in this table

One turbine, four hypothetical thermal masses. The heavier it gets the slower it is (larger τ), and the thirty seconds we see becomes smaller relative to it. That is how we isolate the effect of slowness on its own, and the two policies are the same two hand-written rules throughout.

The slower the component, the more preview is worth: 16.5 → 18.0 → 26.0 percentage points, while H/τ falls from 4.47 to 0.60.

The reason is simple. A slow part is not saved by reacting: you act after you see the temperature, and it goes on climbing for a full minute after you have acted. A fast part responds immediately to anything you do, so it does not need warning in advance.

Then the turbine becomes so heavy that it never reaches the limit at all, and there is nothing left to protect it from.

These same numbers were 0.0, 0.1 and 0.2 at the old 930 K protection limit. Nothing changed in the engine or in the model — only the limit. That is why the limit is quoted with every advantage figure.

And what does an engineer do with this? What we measured says that paying is worth it where H/τ is near one or below, with the highest we have seen at 0.60, and that there is no point paying where the component never reaches its limit at all. We do not offer that as a hard threshold: one curve from one system does not make a rule, and the second system is what will settle it.

Why the examiner should believe us — the honesty record

  1. Every number came out of an actual run
    We do not quote a number that a script does not print. The percentages here are derived from printed columns, and we say what they were derived from.
  2. Thirteen mistakes documented in the open
    Among them: the simulation ran the wrong engine for three weeks — a 2.0 L inline-four instead of a 3.0 L inline-six. Written out in full detail in the project file, not buried.
  3. Two calibrations corrected against the car
    The enrichment map (enrichment — injecting extra petrol not to burn but to cool hot parts) was rebuilt from 175.5 minutes of real driving over nine drives. And the spark map (spark — when the plug fires before the piston arrives) is in two pieces: a fit over eleven measured steady points below about 90 kPa (residual RMS 1.66° against 8.43° for the old map), and above that the knock limit computed inside the model itself — no measurement there, and we say so. It is backed by measuring the retard the car applies on its own: median 0°, 99th percentile 9.8°.
  4. Our own hypothesis failed, and we published the whole table
    We expected the value of preview to grow the more convex the cost function is — the more it punishes a large overshoot harder than a small one. The measurement says the opposite: the advantage fell from 36.8 points at a linear cost to 1.5 points at the fourth power, while the exponential cost gives 26.0. We published the six rows exactly as the script printed them, and did not pick out the ones we liked.
  5. A number we called "validated", then retracted ourselves
    We said a 1.4 % gap between our model and the car proved the two matched. Then we found that this gap compares two channels of the car's own computer and does not pass through our model at all. Delete the entire model and the number does not move. We wrote that into the mistake log rather than quietly keeping the claim.
  6. A checker that opens every document, not just its own data
    verify_docs.py recomputes every published figure from the data itself. Then it opens every document and every code file, compares what is written there against what was computed, and fails naming the file and the line. Last run, 13 September 2026: 33 checks pass and 230 figure mentions agree.
  7. What is not done yet, we say before being asked
    The agent has not been trained — that is Phase C. The Phase D table — three baselines, twenty fixed evaluation episodes, five seeds — has not been produced, and it is the floor of the project, not its ceiling. And the second system (the battery) has not been written: there is no battery.py in the repository.
  8. The protocol we stand on is a fallback, and we say so
    The preferred method for Phase F (H2b) sets the limit at the 80th percentile of the unprotected policy's own trace. In a sustained climb — nine of its twelve minutes at the top — the 80th percentile lands on the peak, so the top three rows saturate at 100 % for both policies. So we show the fixed-limit H2 table instead, and replacing the H2b rule is declared open work.
  9. How we could be proved wrong
    The falsifiable claim: the points from both systems should fall on a single curve against H/τ, whatever system produced them. If the battery's curve does not lie on the engine's, the criterion is wrong or incomplete — and we report that as a result, naming the missing dimension, with actuator authority against disturbance size the likeliest candidate.

Five objections, with an answer ready for each

"The linear theory already covers this"
Partly yes, and we grant it from the first moment.
The theoretical bound proves that the benefit saturates, not what it is worth at a given horizon, and it is confined to linear systems with no hard constraints. We give measured magnitudes where the theory gives existence only.
"Mercedes has been doing this for years"
On trucks, for speed and gears, to save fuel.
That is proof that preview is worth building for one system and one objective, not a description of how the benefit changes. And our question — is it worth it on something that is not a truck, and how do you know before you build — is untouched by a commercial product.
"Why not just run MPC and see?"
Fair question, and it is inside our plan, not outside it.
MPC is one of the baselines we will measure against in the next phase. We have not built it, and there is no MPC code in the repository today. But running it needs a validated model, a chosen horizon and a solver: the whole investment before you know whether it is worth it. The criterion answers earlier, from two numbers on a whiteboard.
"A BSc project does not prove a general law"
True, and we do not claim a law.
We claim a criterion supported so far by one system — the engine — with a second system (a battery) that is Phase E and has not started. If the two curves do not coincide once it is done, the criterion is incomplete and we report that as a result. The honest wording: evidence for a criterion, not proof of a law — and it is written in those words in the abstract.
"Was your literature search exhaustive?"
No, and it is a targeted search whose limits we state ourselves.
It covered three areas: preview control theory, predictive powertrain control, and reinforcement learning for engine calibration. It did not cover process control, robotics, building HVAC or power systems, any of which may carry an equivalent criterion in different vocabulary. Anyone claiming absolute coverage looks naive; anyone who states the limits of their search looks precise.
Say this if a supervisor stops you in the corridorWe are not building a predictive controller. We measure when advance knowledge is worth paying for at all: how many seconds we see ahead (H = 30 s) divided by how many seconds the part we protect needs to heat up (τ = 50 s for us). And the proof that preview alone is responsible: switch it off and our predictive policy falls back onto the reactive one exactly — 548.6 against 548.6.
