Where we are

Where we are, what's next

Seven phases · two done · Phase D, the floor we must clear, not started · 11 September 2026

The simulator was checked against the car on every quantity the car exposes, and it passed; its limits are below. No agent has been trained. Phase D is the floor we must clear to graduate.

175.5 minutes Logged in the car, over 8 drives build_dataset.py
22 points Steady operating points, 30–74 kPa (outside air about 101) build_dataset.py
1.4 % Gap between the two air channels, zero fitted parameters compare_log.py
8 / 11 quantities Inside the published band, the range the references give validate.py

The ratio the whole project turns on

A simulator is a program that imitates the engine step by step, so we can test control strategies without a car and without risk. The engine it imitates is a specific one: the BMW B58B30O1 from a 2023 Toyota GR Supra — a straight-six, meaning six cylinders in a single row, 2997.5 cc.

Preview means the controller — the software deciding what the engine does moment to moment — is told what the road ahead will demand before the car reaches it. We give it thirty seconds' worth. Think of two drivers. One sees a sign reading "6 km climb" and backs off early so the engine goes into the slope cool; the other notices nothing until the car starts feeling heavy. The whole project asks one question: when is that sign worth what it costs to put up?

H is the preview horizon: how many seconds of road we can see ahead. Tau — the Greek letter τ — is the time constant of the part we are protecting: how long it takes to get about two-thirds of the way from its old temperature to its new one. For our turbine, τ is 48 seconds.

If the part reacts far more slowly than we can see ahead, thirty seconds of warning buys nothing: the problem takes ten minutes to build and we would have seen it coming anyway. If it reacts far faster, it has already overheated and recovered before the warning could have changed anything. All the value sits in the middle, where the two timescales are comparable, and nobody has mapped that middle. Mapping it is what this project claims to do. Building a better controller is not.

Here is the version that can be proved wrong. We will build a second system — a battery, whose physics has nothing in common with an engine's — and measure the same thing on it. Plotted against H/τ, the measurements from both systems have to fall on one curve. If they do not, the criterion is wrong or incomplete, and reporting that is a result worth publishing rather than a failure.

The evidence we have today

Logging from the car
175.5 minutes, 8 drives, 6 of them carrying usable samples
All of it read-only, through the OBD-II diagnostic socket, with not one command written back to the car. Six of the nine drives carry usable samples. Two are shorter than a minute and never warm up. A third is a reconnaissance run: we recorded 655 channels at once — a channel is one reading the car publishes, and 655 is almost every one it offers — so the logger could not keep up and a quarter of the samples were lost. That run does carry samples, but it yields no steady operating points, so the 22 points come from five drives. It is also the reason we know which 21 channels to record on every drive since.
Steady operating points
22 distinct points, 30–74 kPa
An operating point is a stretch of steady driving where the model and the car can be compared directly; the "load" at that point is how much air the engine takes in per cycle. Each point is one minute long, and that minute has to be a real minute on the clock with no break in the recording. One whole drive lost every candidate minute it had, because the logger was dropping a quarter of the samples.
The gap between the two air channels (load residual)
1.4 % with zero fitted parameters, derived constant k = 0.829
And 1.1 % if we allow ourselves to hand-tune one constant, k, which converts the car's air-filling reading into the units the model uses: 0.837 tuned, against 0.829 derived from first principles. The direction is the healthy one — one free parameter should always improve a fit, so a better result with no tuning at all would be the suspicious outcome. Either way, both numbers compare two of the car's own readings and neither tests the breathing model. See "No test of the breathing model at everyday, low loads" below.
Validation table
8 of 11 quantities inside the published band
The published band is the range the references and textbooks give for that quantity on an engine like ours. The table regenerates with one command: python validate.py
Reward function check
4 of 4 checks pass, and the neutral policy scores −0.00438
The reward is the score the agent is trained to maximise, and a policy is simply the rule it uses to choose what to do. The neutral policy is an agent that changes nothing and leaves every setting where the car's own engine computer put it, so its score has to come out near zero. If it comes out positive, the reward is paying the agent to do nothing; if it comes out strongly negative, the reward is punishing it for no reason. This is the gate to pass before believing any training curve.
Turbine thermal time constant (τ)
48.0 seconds — measured
The turbine is a small wheel spun by the hot exhaust leaving the engine, and it is the part most at risk when things get hot. Its time constant is how long it takes to get about two-thirds of the way to its new temperature. 48 seconds means you press the pedal now and the turbine keeps heating for the best part of a minute afterwards. This is the τ in H/τ, and the figure calculated from theory, 50.3 seconds, agrees with it.

The preliminary result, and the strongest fact we have

  • 13.4 percentage points of preview advantage Against the baseline, reactive protection cuts damage by 33.8 % and predictive protection by 47.2 %. The gap between them is 13.4 percentage points, measured at a protection limit of 1123 K (850 °C). Damage here is a running total of heat-driven wear — mostly the turbine, with a smaller oil term alongside it — and it is an index with no physical unit, so only the ratios between the figures mean anything.
  • 548.6 against 548.6 Blind the predictive agent to the road ahead and it scores exactly what the reactive controller scores: 548.6 against 548.6. The entire advantage therefore comes from the preview information and from nothing else. The size of that advantage has changed four times, across two engines, two scenarios and two protection triggers. The two numbers have come out equal every single time. That is why we expect the Phase D ablation to hold up.
  • The H2 sweep: how the value moves with the ratio H2 is the second of the sweeps in generality_test.py. We held the preview horizon at 30 seconds and varied the size of the turbine. The advantage rises from 16.5 to 18.0 to 26.0 percentage points as H/τ falls from 4.47 to 0.60. Below that, the turbine is large enough that its temperature never reaches the limit at all, so protection has nothing to do and preview buys nothing. Every figure here is at the 1123 K limit.
  • State the limit with every preview number The same rows read 0.0, 0.1 and 0.2 points at the old 930 K limit. Nothing about the engine changed; only the limit did. A preview number without the limit it was measured against is not a result.

Why 8 of 11 is the expected outcome, not a failure

  • The three that fall outside are known by name Peak exhaust gas temperature at low, steady cruising loads, plus two oil quantities: oil temperature on a sustained climb, and the oil time constant. None of the three is a surprise or a hidden defect, and validation_table.md gives the reason for each.
  • Oil above 107 °C is extrapolation 107 °C is the hottest reading of the oil temperature channel anywhere in the logs, and the sensor downstream of the oil filter reaches 111 °C on that same drive. The published band, 115–140 °C, starts above both. So anything we say about hotter oil rests on the structure of the model, not on anything we measured.
  • The rule Matching the car and falling outside the published band is better than moving a coefficient to close the gap. Do not tune a parameter to make a row in a table pass.

The limits we state plainly in the thesis

No test of the breathing model at everyday, low loads
0 tests
The breathing model is our estimate of how much air enters the engine on each cycle. Both of the pressure sensors this car exposes sit ahead of the throttle — the throttle is the valve the accelerator pedal opens — so we have no reading of the pressure inside the engine to compare a modelled value against. The 1.4 % is no substitute. We forced the breathing model's central number, volumetric efficiency, to 0.50 and then to 1.20, and the residual did not move by as much as 0.001 %.
The comparison against the car covers light load only
30–74 kPa
Air pressure outside the car is about 101 kPa. Under boost — when the turbocharger is forcing extra air in — the engine runs past 200 kPa, so 30 to 74 kPa covers only the low-pressure end of its range. Steady points need steady driving, and steady driving is light-load driving. That is a fact about the road, not a shortcoming in the code.
We have no measurements of the compressor above 0.303 kg/s of corrected flow
Above that limit: 0 measurements
The compressor is the fan on the other end of the turbine's shaft: the turbine spins it, and it forces extra air into the engine. The sensor that measures air flow stops at 1020 kg/h, and then keeps reporting that exact figure as though it were a real reading. We see the same number repeated 517 times across five drives. Above that point we know nothing, and no amount of driving can fill the gap with this sensor.
The radiator is not identifiable on this car
R² = 0.157
The radiator is the part that cools the engine's coolant. Every water-pump channel on this car reads zero, so we cannot tell how much coolant is flowing, and without a flow figure there is no way to calculate how much heat the radiator is removing. We tried a constrained fit over 55 minutes of data. It came back with a negative coefficient for road speed, which would mean that driving faster heats the radiator. That is impossible, so the data simply do not contain the answer. We write it down as a limit and stop.
The percentile threshold rule in Phase F does not survive
3 of 5 rows saturate
We tried setting the protection limit at the 80th percentile of the temperatures recorded in the run itself, which assumes the temperature sits near its peak for only a small part of the time. Our scenario is a sustained climb — nine minutes out of twelve at the top — so the 80th percentile lands on the peak, and the top rows come out with both policies spending 100 % of the time above the limit, which tells us nothing. Until the rule is replaced we use the fixed-limit H2 table instead: preview worth 16.5, then 18.0, then 26.0 percentage points as H/τ falls from 4.47 to 0.60, all of it measured at a fixed protection limit of 1123 K.

The next five steps, in order, with the real time cost

  1. python check_premise.py
    a few minutes
    Confirm the simulation environment works at all. It should print the protection limit, 1123 K (850 °C), then four damage figures: 829.2 with no protection; 548.6 for protection that only reacts once the temperature has already risen; 437.6 for protection that can see the road ahead; and 548.6 for that same predictive controller once we blind it to the road. Read the second and the fourth: they are the same number. Blinding the predictive controller drops it exactly back onto the reactive one, so the whole advantage comes from preview and from nothing else. If those figures do not appear, stop before writing any code.
  2. pip install "stable-baselines3[extra]"
    two minutes
    Without this install, train.py stops in its import block and prints a message saying exactly why. Nothing is broken; the library is simply not there. That is also how we know nothing past those imports has ever been run.
  3. python test_reward.py
    one minute — before training, not after
    One minute now against 4.6 hours wasted later. Run it before you start training, not afterwards. All four checks have to pass, and the neutral policy has to score −0.00438. A training curve computed on a broken reward is worse than no curve at all, because it looks like progress.
  4. python train.py --steps 50000 --seed 0
    about 4.6 hours on one CPU core
    A measured number, not a guess: the simulation alone runs at 19.5 steps per second, and 3.0 steps per second once the learning algorithm — Soft Actor-Critic, or SAC — starts doing its own gradient updates on top. Plan a whole night, not an evening, and expect a poor result; the point of this run is only that it completes. Checkpoints are saved every 10 000 steps, so closing the laptop costs you minutes, not the night.
  5. Ten runs — the input to Phase D
    two nights, run in parallel across five people
    A seed is the random starting number that makes one training run reproducible, so five seeds means five independent runs of the same setup. Five seeds, one per team member, then the same five again with --no-preview. Agree who takes which seed before anyone starts, or you will end up with three copies of seed zero. Even once all ten are done, Phase D itself is still ahead: three baselines as set out in the repository plan, a fixed protocol of 20 evaluation episodes, and a table of medians and interquartile ranges.

Four rules that do not bend

  • Never write to the engine computer (ECU) The engine computer is the box that decides how much fuel to inject and when to fire the spark plugs. The OBD-II port is the diagnostic socket we plug the laptop into. We only ever read from it; we never send it a command. A bad write can damage a real engine that belongs to one of us, and that is outside what a graduation project should risk. If a task looks as though it needs a write, it is the wrong task — say so rather than looking for a way round.
  • No hand edits to data/, logs/raw/, or validation_table.md The data folder and the validation table are produced by a command, so regenerate them rather than editing them. The raw log folder holds actual measurements from the car, and nobody edits those.
  • Do not quote a number that no script prints Run verify_docs.py before writing any number into the thesis. It once found 55 stale numbers still shipping in the documents.
  • Do not change the evaluation set after seeing the results An episode is one simulation run from start to finish. The twenty test episodes are chosen once and never changed: their seeds go into a file in the repository, and that file is treated as read-only from then on. Changing the test set after seeing the results is the one mistake this project cannot recover from.

The summary in four lines

What is missing is not the correctness of the numbers; it is Phase D. Every headline figure here is printed by a single command anyone can re-run. The one thing no script prints is the R² of 0.157 from the radiator fitting attempt, and that is documented with its source in logs/CHANNEL_CENSUS.md. Even the analytical 50.3 seconds from C/UA — heat capacity divided by heat-transfer coefficient — is printed: it is the τ column of the H2 table in generality_test.py.

What is left: ten training runs, two nights each across five people, and one results table.

And if the agent does not beat the baselines? Then we say so plainly. A negative result under a protocol fixed before the experiment is still a defensible result. What would destroy the project is changing the test set after seeing the results, not losing a comparison.

This status is current as of 11 September 2026. One command checks that every figure in it is still true: python verify_docs.py.

Say this if a supervisor stops you in the corridorWe have a simulator checked against a real car over 175.5 minutes of logging and 22 steady operating points between 30 and 74 kPa. What is left is Phase D: an agent that beats the baselines, which are the simple control strategies we compare it against. Then we run the same experiment again with the road ahead hidden from the agent. If the advantage disappears when we hide it, preview is what produced it.
07
The mistake log

Thirteen mistakes already made

Each one is written out in full in CLAUDE.md, with the figure it broke and the figure that replaced it. Read them before you change anything — half of them cost weeks.

This list is not an admission of failure. It is the strongest card the project holds: an examiner who reads a hand-kept log of errors believes every other number you give them.

#The mistakeWhat is left of it
1 The simulation was the wrong engine for three weeks. Geometry() defaulted to a generic 2.0 L inline-four, and b58() — the real 3.0 L inline-six — was an opt-in override that not one call site in the repository ever passed. Torque was 33 % low, air mass 33 % low, exhaust gas temperature 46 °C low. A default that silently picks a different physical system is not a convenience, it is a trap. Pass the geometry explicitly at every call site.
2 The pressure channel is not what its name says. "Intake manifold absolute pressure" on this vehicle is a pre-throttle sensor. At warm idle it reads 13.49 against an ambient of 14.23, where real manifold pressure must be about a third of ambient. Feeding it to the model gives about 75 % air-mass error. Always use plant.map_from_airflow().
3 The guessed baseline ECU was flattering us. It ran a knock integral of 1.4–2.1 — detonating continuously, which no production ECU does. About 90 % of its apparent damage was a knock penalty. A baseline you guessed will flatter you, and the flattery shows up as a large, impressive headline number.
4 The enrichment map was wrong three times, and the third time the variable itself was wrong. Version three was fitted to 17 seconds of high load. With nine drives we had 178 seconds, and at that sample size the correlation between lambda and manifold pressure is +0.23 — weak, and with the wrong sign for a load table. One drive is not evidence. A fit that reproduces the effect can still be built on the wrong variable — check how many samples support it, and check that the variable actually correlates.
5 The reward carried a live hack — twice. A policy that refused to make torque scored +0.285 against neutral's +0.003. It was fixed, then came back after the engine correction, because the first fix had been verified on the four-cylinder in a scenario where the constraint never bound. Delivering the requested torque is the job, not a preference. Run test_reward.py after any change to the reward or the plant or the scenario.
6 A part-load fit extrapolated into boost. A straight line through 11 part-load points, extended to 240 kPa, put the baseline at +21° BTDC — a number no turbo engine survives. Do not extend a fit beyond the data that produced it. Say where it stops.
7 A channel hits its range limit and keeps reporting. "Air mass flow" tops out at exactly 1020.0 kg/h on five separate drives, across 517 samples. Before fitting anything to a channel, check whether it saturated. A flat maximum repeated across drives is the tell.
8 A "60 second window" was neither 60 seconds nor a window. Windows spanned 42.8 to 73.7 seconds, and one contained an internal hole of 3.18 s. The cause was upstream: a drive logged with 655 channels lost 23 % of its duration entirely. Census once with everything, then record the small set for real drives. Twenty channels log cleanly at 4.6 Hz with no dropouts.
9 A knock limiter that failed open, and published MBT as the calibration. In a cell that knocks at every advance in the range, the variable stayed None and the table printed the most knock-prone advance in the row as if it were safe. A limiter that publishes the unlimited value when it cannot find a safe one is worse than no limiter, because the output still looks like a number. If a table is meant to have a shape, check the shape.
10 neutral_action() was not neutral, and was outside the action space. It mapped all five actions to "zero", which put the cooling fan off and the pump at its floor. The test file already knew — it carried its own corrected copy locally. A fix that lives in the test file is not a fix. Correct the helper itself; everything downstream inherits the bug.
11 Retired figures survived in the prose after the data moved on. Six places at first. Then, on 11 September, 55 stale figures in a single pass — every one of them with a matching green line in the checker's own output, because the checker compared the data against constants written inside itself and never opened a document. A checker that only checks itself is not a checker. verify_docs.py now opens every tracked .md and .py and compares what is written there against what the data computes.
12 A residual that could not see the thing it was said to validate. We called 1.4 % "the simulator matches the car". Delete the breathing model entirely and the number does not move: 1.3740 % without it against 1.3737 % with it. A residual computed through an inversion of the same model cannot test that model. Before calling any residual a validation, perturb the parameter it supposedly validates and check the number moves. It costs one run.
13 The charge temperature was a compressor outlet. The channel named "Intake air temperature before throttle valve" reads 149 °C under boost. The B58 carries its charge cooler inside the manifold, downstream of the throttle. We had been blaming the breathing model for a +23.7 % gap; it was the temperature, and the model was innocent. Third channel on this car that is not what its name says. Treat every channel name as a hypothesis.
Say it out loud before anyone finds it A student who reports a result that went against them is trusted on everything else they claim.
08
Glossary

Project Glossary

Thirty-eight terms that all five of us must be able to explain

This is not a dictionary. Every term here comes with the reason it exists in this project, and thirty-eight of them are terms each of us must be able to explain to an outsider in a minute.

Before you start: the engine in plain English

A cylinder is a closed tube with a piston moving up and down inside it. The crankshaft is the shaft that turns that up-and-down motion into rotation, and its rotation is what eventually reaches the wheels.

On every cycle, air enters the cylinder through the intake manifold — the pipe that distributes air to the cylinders. Fuel is injected into that air, and the spark plug ignites the mixture. The burning gas expands and drives the piston down. That is the only push the engine ever gives.

Because everything here is tied to where the piston is rather than to the clock, we measure time in degrees of crankshaft rotation, not in seconds. Top Dead Centre (TDC) is the highest point the piston reaches, and it is the zero that every angle in this glossary is measured from.

"Load" means how much air enters the cylinder per cycle. We measure it as the air pressure in the intake manifold, in kilopascals (kPa). Atmospheric pressure is about 101 kPa; below that is part load, above it is boost. The throttle is a plate that opens and closes to set how much air gets in, and it is what the accelerator pedal moves.

One last word that will come up constantly. We call every signal we read out of the car's computer a channel — "air mass flow", say, or "coolant temperature". And the rule we have now paid for three times: a channel name is a hypothesis, not a fact.

Before the list itself, a word on how to read it. The full reference holds about ninety terms, and memorising all of them is the wrong goal. Thirty-eight entries here are marked with a star, and those are the ones all five of us must be able to explain to an outsider in roughly a minute. For the rest, recognising them and knowing where to find them is enough.

Count them yourself if you like. Project_Vocabulary.pdf says twenty while carrying thirty-three stars, and this version carries thirty-eight after the missing entries were added. The number written here is what the page actually shows, not what an older file claims.

Every entry has a definition and then a "why it matters" line. The second line is the important one: it separates someone who understood the project from someone who memorised a dictionary. In the individual defence — the part of the oral exam where each of us is questioned alone — "that part is my teammate's" is a bad answer.

Say them out loud before the session: H∞ is "H-infinity", MBT is "em-bee-tee", MFB50 is "em-eff-bee fifty", BMEP is "bee-em-ee-pee", BSFC is "bee-ess-eff-see", MPC is "em-pee-see", SAC rhymes with "sack", λ is "lambda", τ is "tau", and H/τ is "H over tau".

0 terms

Combustion and knock

End gas

The flame front is the surface between what has burned and what has not, and it travels through the mixture as a wave. The end gas is what is left ahead of it, not yet reached by the flame. It heats up without any flame reaching it. The burning gas behind it expands and compresses it, and compressing any gas raises its temperature — the same reason a bicycle pump gets hot when you pump up a tyre.

Why it matters here · This is the gas that ignites by itself in knock, so every knock equation is written about its state, not about the average temperature of the whole cylinder. That is why it is defined before knock is: knock does not mean anything without it.

Knock★ viva

Healthy combustion is a single flame that leaves the spark plug and creeps through the mixture until it has consumed all of it. In knock, the mixture furthest from the plug does not wait for the flame. The advancing flame compresses and heats it until it ignites by itself, all at once. Picture a sheet of paper burning steadily from one edge; knock is a tin of petrol going off in a single bang. The result is a pressure wave that crosses the cylinder at the speed of sound and hammers the piston crown. Sustained knock puts holes in pistons.

Why it matters here · The heaviest term in the whole project. Knock is the constraint every other decision defends, and it is the direct reason we never write anything to the car's computer.

Spark advance★ viva

How early the plug fires, measured in degrees of crankshaft rotation still to go before the piston reaches Top Dead Centre. We fire early because the flame needs one to two milliseconds to spread: fire at Top Dead Centre itself and peak pressure would arrive far too late, with the piston already well down the bore. It is like passing a ball ahead of a running player rather than to where he is standing. The aim is for peak pressure to land just after Top Dead Centre, where the piston can take work from it on the way down.

Why it matters here · The agent's first lever. Every extra degree of advance means more torque — up to the MBT limit, defined in the next entry, and no further — and always more risk of knock. That is the basic trade in an engine.

MBT (minimum spark advance for best torque)★ viva

The timing that gives maximum torque. Fire later than it and part of the expansion goes to waste without doing work; fire earlier and peak pressure arrives while the piston is still coming up, so the pressure pushes the piston against its own motion — like pushing a swing while it is still coming towards you.

Why it matters here · At high load you cannot reach MBT at all, because the knock limit arrives first and stops you short of it. This conflict between "maximum torque" and "no knock" is exactly what engine calibration means, and it is the trade our agent learns.

MFB50 (mass fraction burned 50 %)★ viva

The crank angle at which half the fuel mass has burned. At MBT timing it lands between 8 and 10 degrees after Top Dead Centre — in almost any engine, at any speed and load. That makes it a yardstick that works on any engine, with no recalibration from one engine to the next.

Why it matters here · Our single best check that the combustion model is sound. The model gives 8.5 degrees at 2500 rpm and 60 kPa manifold pressure — about 60 per cent of atmospheric, so part load. That is inside the published range, so we can be reasonably confident that the Wiebe function's coefficients (four entries down) are sensible.

Lambda λ★ viva

The actual air-to-fuel ratio divided by the ideal one, which for petrol is 14.7:1 by mass. λ = 1 means exactly enough air, below one means a mixture rich in fuel, above one means a lean mixture.

Why it matters here · We use lambda to control temperature, not to make power. We calibrated the baseline's enrichment map against lambda values measured on the car itself.

Enrichment★ viva

Deliberately running the engine with more fuel than the air can burn, at high load. The extra fuel goes in as a liquid and does not all burn; evaporating it takes heat out of the charge — the air and fuel trapped in the cylinder — the way sweat cools your skin. The result is cooler exhaust, at the cost of wasted fuel.

Why it matters here · Eight drives told us this car does not enrich by load, but by engine speed and by how long it has been held above a 180 kPa threshold. Lambda's correlation with manifold pressure is only +0.23 — weak, and pointing the wrong way for a load table: higher pressure goes with a leaner mixture, not a richer one. The real correlations are with engine speed (−0.56) and with how long the engine has been held above the threshold (−0.47).

Douaud–Eyzat induction-time integral★ viva

How we model knock. Auto-ignition is a race, not a threshold: at any instant the end gas has an induction time τ — the delay before it would ignite on its own if it were held at its present temperature and pressure. At each instant we add the fraction of that delay used up, and the running total is what we call the knock integral, KI = ∫dt/τ. If it reaches one before the flame arrives, knock has happened. A warning about the symbol: this τ is an induction time measured in thousandths of a second, and it is not the τ in H/τ, which is a thermal time constant measured in tens of seconds.

Why it matters here · It is what makes knock a graded quantity the agent can learn from, rather than a switch that is either on or off. Temperature enters it exponentially, so ten extra degrees cost far more than one.

Wiebe function

An empirical S-shaped curve describing the fraction of fuel burned against crank angle.

Why it matters here · It is what turns spark timing into a heat-release schedule. Without it, changing the timing would have no effect inside the model at all.

Knock retard

The degrees the car's computer has pulled out of spark advance after detecting knock. Available as a manufacturer-specific read channel.

Why it matters here · We measured it ourselves: median 0 degrees, 99th percentile 9.8 degrees, over 10 896 samples once gearshifts and torque cuts are excluded. Our baseline caps it at 12 degrees, which makes it slightly conservative rather than a strawman built to lose. Do not use the raw difference between the ECU's target ignition angle and its actual one: unfiltered it reaches 45 degrees, and that is a deliberate torque cut at the moment of a gearshift, not knock.

Work and torque

Torque

The turning effort the crankshaft delivers. It is what the driver is actually asking for when the pedal goes down, and power is simply torque multiplied by rotational speed.

Why it matters here · Delivering the requested torque is the job, not a preference. Treating it as a preference with a tradeable weight is what opened our reward hack twice (see it in the reinforcement-learning section): the agent worked out that the reward it lost by refusing to make torque was smaller than the reward it gained by avoiding damage, so it refused.

Mean effective pressure (MEP)

The work the engine gives in a cycle divided by the displacement — the volume all the pistons sweep in one cycle, the thing the advertising calls "3.0-litre engine capacity". The result has units of pressure, because work = force × distance and force ÷ area = pressure. The point of the division is to compare two engines of different sizes fairly.

Why it matters here · Two engines of different sizes cannot be compared on work per cycle, only on work per litre — which is exactly what this gives you. But do not use it to rescue our old numbers: everything computed on the four-cylinder before 8 September is void, and it gets regenerated by the scripts, not rescaled.

BMEP (brake mean effective pressure)★ viva

What actually reaches the crankshaft: the gas work on the piston, minus the pumping work (what the engine spends drawing air in and pushing exhaust out), minus friction. Torque is directly proportional to it, so if you know one you know the other.

Why it matters here · This is the load measure we use to compare our engine against published figures. A modern turbocharged engine peaks at about 20 to 22 bar (one bar is roughly one atmosphere). That is the plausibility ceiling on our own numbers: if the model reports forty bar, the model is wrong — our engine is not exceptional.

BSFC (brake specific fuel consumption)★ viva

Grams of fuel per kilowatt-hour of work — how much fuel the engine costs you for each unit of work it gives, so the lower the number the better. About 235 grams at the best point in a good engine, and far worse at idle (running with no load).

Why it matters here · There is a trap in it: it divides by power, so it is undefined at zero torque. That is why our reward uses fuel mass flow instead.

Air path and heat

MAP (manifold absolute pressure)

The pressure inside the intake manifold. Below about 101 kPa means vacuum, above it means boost. It is the primary load axis of any engine map.

Why it matters here · The channel with that name on our car does not measure it. Its sensor sits before the throttle rather than after it, so it reads the air before the throttle has restricted it. Feeding it to the model gave an air-mass error of about 75 per cent, so we now work manifold pressure out from the air-mass-flow channel instead, by running the breathing model backwards.

Turbocharger · wastegate★ viva

Exhaust gas on its way out spins a turbine wheel, and the turbine drives a compressor that forces more air into the same cylinder. More air means more fuel can be burned, so an engine of the same size makes more torque. The wastegate routes part of the exhaust around the turbine instead of through it, and that is what sets how much boost there is.

Why it matters here · The agent does not operate the wastegate. It writes a trim on manifold pressure (−40 to +15 kPa) — a trim is an adjustment added to what the baseline already commands. An inner PI loop turns that trim into a wastegate command, and in the real car the wastegate is what that loop acts on. ("Duty cycle", a phrase you will read often below, means the percentage of the time an actuator is switched on.) The turbine housing — the metal shell the turbine runs inside — is the component this whole project protects, and it owns the τ in H/τ.

EGT (exhaust gas temperature)★ viva

The temperature of the gas leaving the cylinder. Above about 950 °C the turbine blades and the catalytic converter are at risk.

Why it matters here · It rises when spark is retarded and falls with enrichment, so it is the first place the agent's levers show up. Keep four temperatures in our standard scenario apart. Exhaust GAS peaks at 1058.6 °C, and the environment carries a gas constraint at 1223 K (949.9 °C). The turbine housing METAL reaches 879 °C at baseline, against a metal protection limit of 1123 K (849.9 °C). Note that the baseline metal temperature already sits above its limit — that is where the damage we are trying to reduce comes from.

Volumetric efficiency

How much air the engine actually swallows, relative to what it would swallow if its cylinders filled completely with air at the density measured at the intake inlet. In practice: a measure of how well the engine breathes. It peaks in the middle of the speed range, where the pressure waves in the intake runner line up with the moment the valves open.

Why it matters here · There is no part-load test of it on this car at all, because both pressure channels sit before the throttle, so there is nothing to compare against. Do not cite the 1.4 per cent residual as if it covered this: volumetric efficiency cancels out of both sides of the equation, so the number does not move however much we change it — that is mistake 12 in the project's mistake log (CLAUDE.md).

Charge temperature

The temperature of the air that actually enters the cylinder, after the intercooler — a heat exchanger that cools the air back down once the compressor has heated it. On the B58 — the 3.0-litre six-cylinder engine in the car we log — that cooler sits inside the intake manifold itself, which is after the throttle, not before it.

Why it matters here · Mistake 13: the channel named "before throttle valve" is a compressor outlet, and it reads 149 °C under boost. Replacing it with our own model shrank the gap between the manifold pressure we compute from air flow and the boost pressure the car reports, from +23.7 per cent to +3.0 per cent. It also cleared the breathing model, which we had blamed wrongly for two weeks.

Lumped capacitance★ viva

Treating a whole metal mass as if it were one uniform temperature with one heat capacity, instead of tracking heat point by point inside it: C·dT/dt = heat in − UA·(T − ambient). Here C is the heat capacity — how much energy it takes to raise the mass by one degree — and UA is how readily it loses heat to its surroundings. Picture a leaky bucket: C is the size of the bucket, UA is the size of the hole.

Why it matters here · Coarse, but exactly what we need: we care how fast heat piles up, not how it spreads through the metal. Our network has three nodes only: block and coolant, oil, and the turbine housing.

Time constant τ = C / UA★ viva

The time a thermal node takes to cover 63 per cent of the way to its new equilibrium. And 63 is not an arbitrary number: what remains of the gap decays exponentially, so after one time constant 1/e of it is left. The mental picture is a mug of coffee cooling on a desk: it closes most of the gap to room temperature quickly and the last of it slowly, and how long that takes is set by the size of the mug and how well it loses heat — not by the moment you put it down.

Why it matters here · The central quantity in the whole project, and the denominator of H/τ. For our turbine housing, the analytical C/UA gives 50.3 seconds and a step response in the model gives 48.0 seconds, at 3000 rpm, 140 kPa and 108 kW. Both come from the model rather than from a measurement on the car, and both sit inside the published 40-to-120-second band.

Thermostat

A valve that routes coolant around the radiator instead of through it below about 88 °C.

Why it matters here · It is why coolant temperature stays between 88 and 97 °C instead of moving freely, and the model gives 94.5 °C in the regulating state, which is the value validate.py checks. It is also the first of two reasons we cannot identify the radiator parameters on this car — identify meaning work them out from measured data. The thermostat is regulating for 88 to 99 per cent of the driving time (a fraction of the time, not a temperature), so we almost never see the cooling system respond freely. The second reason is the channels: every water-pump channel reads zero, and so do "Actual value of electric fan" and "Duty cycle electric fan". "Setpoint electric fan" is live, but it sits at 28–31 per cent for a whole drive, so there is nothing in it to identify from.

Arrhenius relation

The exponential relation between temperature and reaction rate. The well-known rule that comes out of it is that the rate of damage roughly doubles for every extra ten degrees.

Why it matters here · Our damage model is built on it: exp((turbine temperature − 1123)/45). That is why it gives the agent a smooth slope to follow everywhere, instead of a blunt threshold, and it is where the 1123 K protection limit itself came from.

Control theory

Disturbance

Everything that affects the system and that the controller cannot change: road gradient, air temperature, the driver's torque demand.

Why it matters here · "Preview" for us means seeing exactly this before it arrives, not seeing our own actions. The sustained-climb scenario — a 12 per cent grade at 110 km/h in 42 °C air — is our standard disturbance, and every number in the project is measured on it.

Preview control★ viva

Control that uses advance knowledge of what is coming — a disturbance or a target — rather than current and past measurements alone. Think of a driver who sees the climb ahead and changes down before reaching it, against one who waits until he feels the car slow and only then acts. It is a mature field with decades of theory and practice behind it.

Why it matters here · We do not claim to have invented it, and anyone who says otherwise in the defence loses. What we claim is a measure of when acquiring the future information is worth it at all.

H∞★ viva

A framework for designing robust controllers — robustness here meaning that the guarantee still holds even if your model has described the system wrongly. The H is for Hardy space (whose details you do not need), and the ∞ means you take the worst case across all frequencies rather than the average.

Why it matters here · It gives a guarantee rather than an expectation, which is why it is the standard tool when robustness matters more than average performance. The closest prior work to our claim is a preview result stated in this framework, so we need to know it well enough to say precisely where we differ from it.

Regret★ viva

A term borrowed from online learning — "online" here means decisions taken one after another as the data arrives, and has nothing to do with the internet. Regret is the gap between your controller's performance and that of a reference that had more information than you, and the reference here is a controller that sees the whole disturbance in advance.

Why it matters here · The statement "regret goes to zero as preview grows" is almost exactly the question we are asking. The precise difference you should be able to state: the prior work proves that the convergence exists for linear systems, without giving its rate.

Non-causal controller

A "causal" controller is one that depends on nothing but the past and the present — which is every real controller. A non-causal one uses information from the future: physically impossible, but a useful theoretical reference that bounds the best achievable with perfect foresight. (This sense has nothing to do with "causal identification" in the method section — one word, two meanings.)

Why it matters here · It is the ruler regret is measured against: how much we lost relative to something that sees everything. In our work the MPC baseline plays that role — see it three entries down.

Spectral radius

The largest absolute eigenvalue of the closed-loop system matrix — "closed-loop" meaning the whole loop, plant and controller together, not the plant alone. In discrete time, less than one means stability, and its value governs how fast the system forgets its past.

Why it matters here · The discrete-time cousin of the time constant. A regret bound that decays with the spectral radius is the linear-systems counterpart of our H/τ claim, so we have to say precisely how we differ from it rather than ignore it.

LTI (linear time-invariant)

A system whose behaviour is linear and does not change over time. Almost all of classical control theory assumes it.

Why it matters here · Ours are not: the knock and temperature limits are hard constraints, and the damage costs are exponential. This is exactly where linear theory goes quiet, and it is where our contribution sits.

MPC (model predictive control)★ viva

At every step: simulate the future with a model over a fixed horizon, pick the best sequence of actions, execute only the first of them, then repeat.

Why it matters here · The industry-standard way of using preview. For us it is an oracle baseline: it plans against the true model with perfect preview, to establish the ceiling on what is achievable at all.

Prediction horizon

How far ahead in time an MPC controller plans at each step.

Why it matters here · Industrial practice sets it to cover roughly the settling time of the plant — the thing being controlled — running open loop, with no controller. That is the popular, unmeasured version of the criterion we are trying to measure properly. Do not confuse it with the preview horizon H. The prediction horizon is the span MPC simulates inside its own head with its model; the preview horizon is the span over which real information about the future arrives. The first is a design choice, the second is the independent variable of this whole thesis.

Setpoint

The target the controller is trying to reach and hold.

Why it matters here · Our agent adjusts setpoints; it does not drive actuators directly. That is exactly what "supervisory" means in the project's name, and it is also what makes it transferable to another system.

ECU / DME★ viva

The computer that runs the engine. BMW calls its unit the DME, short for Digitale Motor Elektronik.

Why it matters here · It is the baseline we compare ourselves against, not an opponent to be beaten by a trick. Being locked out of writing to it costs us nothing, because we never intended to write to it: read-only, without a single exception — no flashing, no transmitting on the car's bus, no tuning.

OBD-II · CAN bus · PID

The socket under the dashboard (OBD-II, on-board diagnostics), the internal network behind it (the CAN bus, controller area network), and the numbered parameter identifiers (PIDs) you quote to request data. Note the clash: a PID here is a data identifier, nothing to do with a PID controller.

Why it matters here · All of our real data came from here: 175.5 minutes across nine drives. Remember the rule: a channel name is a hypothesis, not a fact — three channels on this car turned out not to be what their names said.

Reinforcement learning

MDP (Markov decision process)★ viva

The standard formulation: states, actions, transitions, rewards, and the Markov property — that the future depends on the present state alone, not on the whole history.

Why it matters here · Our state has to be complete enough for that property to hold. That is why the three temperatures and the preview channels are in the observation, not just engine speed and load.

CMDP (constrained MDP)★ viva

A Markov decision process with cost functions and budgets alongside the reward. You maximise return within the set of policies that respect the constraints.

Why it matters here · We use it because some things should not have a price. "How much is a lost newton-metre worth in fuel?" is a question with no defensible answer, and a constraint makes the driver's demand a requirement rather than a preference.

Lagrange multiplier λ★ viva

It turns a constrained problem into an unconstrained one, but with an exchange rate that is learned rather than guessed. Read it as a penalty coefficient with a feedback loop.

Why it matters here · Violate the constraint and λ climbs until the policy stops violating it; stay comfortably inside it and λ decays towards zero. In practice it means you will never hand-tune a penalty weight again. A warning about the symbol: this λ is not the air-fuel lambda — one symbol in two fields, and you will hear both in the same session, so if it gets confusing say "Lagrange multiplier" in full.

SAC (Soft Actor-Critic)★ viva

Our algorithm: off-policy reinforcement learning for continuous control. An actor proposes the actions, twin critics estimate their value, and a bonus on randomness itself (entropy bonus) keeps the policy stochastic rather than deterministic.

Why it matters here · It suits us for three reasons: our actions are continuous to begin with, its sample efficiency is high because every transition is reused out of the replay buffer, and the entropy term stops it collapsing early onto a single strategy.

Entropy bonus α★ viva

A reward given to the policy for staying random instead of becoming deterministic, with its coefficient tuned automatically towards a target entropy.

Why it matters here · In a reward landscape with a sharp cliff in it, like knock, this is exactly what you want. A deterministic policy finds one good angle and then stops trying anything else, so it never discovers how close to the edge it was.

Discount factor γ

How much the future weighs against the present: a reward n steps away is multiplied by γ to the power n. The closer γ is to one, the longer the horizon the policy effectively cares about.

Why it matters here · Our actual value is 0.99, which is the stable-baselines3 default, because train.py never passes gamma at all. The vocabulary reference says 0.98 and justifies it by matching the turbine time constant — a claim written on paper and not implemented in the code. Either we pass it for real, or we delete the justification.

Reward shaping★ viva

Designing the reward function so that the behaviour you want really is the highest-scoring one, and not merely the behaviour you had in mind while writing it.

Why it matters here · This is where most of the real work goes, and where most of the silent failures come from. Our reward is relative to the baseline, so zero means exactly "identical to the factory calibration".

Reward hacking★ viva

The agent finding a loophole: technically maximising your reward while doing something absurd or useless.

Why it matters here · It happened to us twice. A policy that simply refused to make torque scored +0.285 against neutral's +0.003, and the same loophole reopened once the engine was corrected. We closed it with a 5 per cent tolerance band that carries no penalty at all inside it, and a penalty that rises very steeply outside it (a steep hinge). No saving in damage can now buy a sustained shortfall in torque: that same torque-refusing policy scores −0.280 today.

Slew limit

A ceiling on how far an action may move in one step — for us 1.5 degrees of spark, 10 kPa of pressure trim, and a quarter of the range for the fan.

Why it matters here · Without it, SAC produces policies that swing across the full range every step. They score well and are physically absurd, and the plots that come out of them leave the thesis indefensible in front of an examiner.

Surrogate model★ viva

A learned, fast approximation of a slow simulator, trained on data the simulator itself generates.

Why it matters here · Not built yet; it is a step after Phase D. Its only purpose is speed: we train on it because it is fast, and we score on the physics because the physics is the truth.

Model exploitation★ viva

When an agent optimises against a learned model, it is not looking for good actions; it is looking for actions the model believes are good. Wherever the model is optimistically wrong, that looks exactly like discovery.

Why it matters here · The second heaviest term: this is the failure mode that would void every result in the project if it happened. Spectacular numbers inside the surrogate, collapse on the physics. Hence the rule, set out under "Tier 0 / Tier 1 / Tier 2" below: train on the fast surrogate, score on the physics.

Ensemble disagreement★ viva

Train several models with different initialisations and samples, then measure how much they disagree. Inside the training distribution they agree closely; outside it they diverge.

Why it matters here · That divergence is our uncertainty signal, and it is the only way a model gets to say "I do not know". It is the practical shield against model exploitation.

Method and statistics

Ablation★ viva

Removing one component and re-running with everything else held fixed, to measure what that component specifically was contributing.

Why it matters here · Our central experimental tool. Zeroing the preview channels is the ablation that answers the main question: it drops the predictive policy's damage score to 548.6, exactly the reactive policy's 548.6. The whole gap was preview and nothing else.

Counterfactual

What would have happened if a different action had been taken in the same state.

Why it matters here · Car logs contain none of these: in each state, the car only ever did one thing. That is why we need a physical model to generate training data, rather than simply driving more.

Causal identification★ viva

Whether the data can tell the effect of one variable apart from the effect of everything correlated with it. If two columns always move together, there is no way to separate their effects.

Why it matters here · In a standard car, spark and lambda are deterministic functions of speed, load and temperature. Lay the log out as a table and the "action" columns are computed from the "state" columns — so there is no way to separate the effect of one from the other, however much data you gather. That is why more logging makes the dataset bigger without making the problem any more solvable — the most important correction to our original plan.

Seed

The starting value of the random number generator, and the thing that makes a run exactly repeatable.

Why it matters here · Deep reinforcement-learning results vary enormously between seeds. That is why we run five seeds per configuration, one per member, and never report a single run.

IQR (interquartile range)

The distance between the 25th and the 75th percentile — where the middle half of the results sits.

Why it matters here · We report the median and the interquartile range rather than the mean and the standard deviation, because reinforcement-learning return distributions are skewed and heavy-tailed, and the mean hides that.

Dimensionless number

A ratio whose units have cancelled by division, so the number means the same thing in any system, whatever its size or its material. The famous example is the Reynolds number. One number tells you whether a flow is smooth or turbulent, in a water pipe or over an aircraft wing, and it means the same thing in both, however different everything else about them is.

Why it matters here · H/τ is our dimensionless number. Having no units is exactly what lets a result travel from a combustion engine to a battery pack, and it is the basis of Phase E.

Our own vocabulary

Plant★ viva

The control-engineering word for the thing being controlled.

Why it matters here · We have one working today, the engine, and a second planned: the battery pack, which is Phase E and does not exist yet (there is no battery.py at all). The second is not a luxury. One plant cannot show that the criterion is general; two can either show it or refute it — and a refutation is a result too, which we would publish.

Tier 0 / Tier 1 / Tier 2★ viva

Tier 0 is the physical model and it defines the truth. Tier 1 is the set of surrogate models and it provides the speed. Tier 2 is the supervisory agent, and it is our contribution.

Why it matters here · The rule that keeps the work honest: train on Tier 1 because it is fast, score on Tier 0 because it is the truth. Mixing the two tiers is model exploitation itself.

Baseline★ viva

The factory calibration, untouched: the car's own computer making its usual decisions, with no intervention from us. In the simulator we represent it with the BaselineECU class, and it is the opponent in every comparison we run.

Why it matters here · Every number in the project is relative to it: "damage cut 47.2 per cent" means against it, and a reward of zero means "exactly identical to it". Anyone who reads a number of ours without asking "relative to what?" has read it wrong.

Standard scenario★ viva

The disturbance all our numbers are measured on: a 12 per cent grade at 110 km/h in 42 °C air, after three minutes of level running. It asks for about 297 Nm, which is 59 per cent of the engine's peak torque.

Why it matters here · Chosen because the constraint actually bites in it — the turbine temperature really does reach its limit. The old scenario (10 per cent at 90 km/h) was too light to stress the six-cylinder, so no trade between torque and damage ever appeared. The premise check runs it for twelve minutes, nine of them on the climb. And Phase D will fix twenty evaluation episodes and then never change them again.

The five actions★ viva

Everything the agent can change: a spark-advance trim (−8 to +4 degrees), a lambda trim (−0.15 to +0.06), a manifold-pressure trim (−40 to +15 kPa) executed by an inner PI loop, then the cooling-fan duty cycle (0 to 1) and the coolant-pump duty cycle (0.3 to 1).

Why it matters here · The first three are trims added to what the baseline commands, so their neutral is zero. The last two are absolute values, not trims, and their neutral is whatever the baseline runs, not zero — which is exactly mistake 10, the one that switched the fan off and called it "neutral".

Neutral action

The five-action vector that reproduces the factory controller's behaviour exactly — not the vector of zeros. Its reward has to be very close to zero; if it is not, the environment itself is broken, not the policy.

Why it matters here · The acceptance test for the whole environment, and it scores −0.00438 today. Note mistake 10: the fix lived in the test file while the exported function stayed wrong — and a fix that lives in the test file is not a fix.

Baseline-relative reward

We score the agent by how much it improves on the factory controller, not by an absolute value. The comparison is at the same operating point — the same speed, load and temperature — otherwise the agent looks better merely for having been measured in easier conditions.

Why it matters here · It makes every term of the reward dimensionless and of order one, so the preference weights actually control the trade. And it gives zero a precise meaning: exactly identical to the factory calibration.

Damage rate

An accumulated Arrhenius-style cost on the turbine and oil temperatures and on the knock integral, instead of a threshold that is either crossed or not.

Why it matters here · The form used is exp((turbine temperature − 1123)/45) + 0.4·exp((oil temperature − 408)/12) + 40·max(0, KI − 0.85)², one term for the turbine, one for the oil and one for knock. It gives a smooth slope everywhere, and it makes ten degrees hotter far worse than one degree. If you change the damage model, change the protection limit with it, because they are the same number.

TURB_PROTECT_K = 1123 K★ viva

The temperature at which the protection policies start to intervene: 1123 K, or 849.9 °C. It is the knee of the damage term — the point where the curve turns sharply upwards, the turbine term reaches one, and damage stops being negligible.

Why it matters here · Not a hand-tuned number and not a percentile of one episode, but a statement about the component itself, which is why it transfers between scenarios. It lives as a single constant in engine_env that check_premise and generality_test both import, so the two experiments cannot report two different limits. It was 930 K in the days of the wrong engine, and at that limit the preview advantage read 0.0, 0.1 and 0.2 points — same physics, different limit.

Preview horizon H★ viva

How far ahead in time the controller can see the coming disturbance. For us that is 30 seconds, and the agent is shown the disturbance at four points inside it: 2, 5, 15 and 30 seconds ahead.

Why it matters here · The independent variable of the whole thesis. Everything we measure is how the value of preview changes with this number, relative to the slowness of the component we are protecting.

Dominant constrained state

The accumulating quantity whose limit the controller is actually defending. Turbine housing temperature in the engine; cell temperature in the battery pack.

Why it matters here · Choosing it correctly is what makes τ mean anything. Choose the wrong state and the criterion measures nothing — the ratio becomes a number about nothing.

H / τ★ viva

The preview horizon divided by the time constant of the dominant constrained state. A ratio with no units, so it means the same thing in the engine and in the battery.

Why it matters here · The heaviest entry in the whole list: this is the project's contribution. The claim is that this ratio alone predicts the value of preview, regardless of domain and actuators. The current measurement, all of it at the 1123 K (849.9 °C) protection limit: the predictive policy beats the reactive one by 16.5, then 18.0, then 26.0 percentage points of damage reduction, as H/τ falls from 4.47 to 0.60. Below that the constraint stops binding, neither policy touches it, and there is nothing left for preview to buy. Not one of those numbers is ever quoted without the limit it was measured at.

Preview advantage (also called the preview edge)

How far the predictive policy beats the reactive one, in percentage points of damage reduction. Both have the same actuators; they differ only in when they act.

Why it matters here · Currently 13.4 points at the 1123 K protection limit: the reactive policy cuts damage 33.8 per cent and the predictive one 47.2 per cent. Never quote this number without the limit it was measured at.

Relative air filling

One of BMW's channels, saying how much air entered the cylinder relative to a defined reference state (DIN: 1013 mbar and 0 °C) rather than to today's weather. It is one of the two quantities the residual compares; the other is measured air mass flow.

Why it matters here · The residual is 1.4 per cent with a derived constant k = 0.829 and zero fitted parameters, and 1.1 per cent with a fitted k = 0.837 and one parameter. Quote the derived one, and say that it costs nothing. Read mistake 12 before calling it validation: it is a consistency check between two of the car's own channels, and it never sees the breathing model at all. What it does earn is two things. It pinned the meaning of the channel at the DIN reference, and it is sensitive to displacement — the derived constant gives 48.1 per cent if it is forced onto the wrong 2.0-litre engine, so it would have caught mistake 1 on its first day.

Validated envelope

What we actually compared against the car: 22 steady operating points between 30 and 74 kPa, extracted from 175.5 minutes across nine drives, six of which carry usable samples.

Why it matters here · Above that — the boosted region — is compared against published references rather than against the car, because steady driving is light-load driving by its nature. Every engine number on this page inherits that limit, so say it before the examiner asks you about it.

Read-only · locked ECU★ viva

The hard constraint of the project: we never write anything to the car's computer. No flashing, no transmitting on the car's bus, no tuning — read-only OBD-II logging, without a single exception.

Why it matters here · Usually the first thing anyone asks about, and the answer is ready: this is not a concession, it is a design condition. All our results come from a simulator calibrated on the car's readings, and the car is a data source, not a control target. If a task appears to require writing to the car, it is the wrong task.

Built vs planned

Built today: the physical model, the thermal network, the training environment, the baseline, and the premise check with hand-written policies. Planned: training the agent (Phase C — nothing has been trained yet), the baselines and the ablation (D), the battery (E), and the surrogate model.

Why it matters here · Say this out loud in any presentation. Every present tense on this page about the agent, the battery or the surrogate model is a plan, not a result, and confusing the two is the fastest way to lose an examiner's trust.

The three that carry the most weight

  • Knock — The constraint every other decision in the project defends. If the examiner asks you one physics question, this is it. Explain it with the end gas and the pressure wave, not with the word "noise".
  • Model exploitation — The failure mode that would void every result in the project if it happened. The answer to have ready: we train on Tier 1 because it is fast, we score on Tier 0 because it is the truth, and we measure uncertainty with ensemble disagreement.
  • H/τ — The contribution itself, and the reason this is a project and not an exercise. If you cannot explain this ratio to an outsider in a minute, you cannot explain the project. Always state the protection limit it was measured at alongside it.
The glossary in one lineKnock is the mixture igniting on its own before the flame reaches it, and it is the constraint every other decision in this project defends. H/τ is the preview horizon divided by the time constant of the component we protect — that ratio is our contribution. We never quote a preview advantage without the 1123 K protection limit it was measured against.
Appendix A
Day one

Run it on your own machine

Five commands. If you get the same numbers, the environment works and you can trust everything else in the repository.

  1. Set up the environment · 2 minutes
    python -m venv .venv, then .venv\Scripts\activate on Windows, then pip install -r requirements.txt. You need Python 3.11 or newer.
  2. python check_premise.py · a few minutes
    What you must see in the damage column: 829.2 · 548.6 · 437.6 · 548.6. If those appear, the environment is sound. If they do not, stop and find out why before writing any code.
  3. python validate.py · 4 minutes
    Regenerates the validation table. Expect 8 of 11 quantities inside the published band — that is not a failure, and the three outside are explained in validation_table.md.
  4. python verify_docs.py · 20 seconds
    Recomputes every published figure from the shipped data, then opens every tracked .md and .py and compares what is written there against what it computed. Run it before quoting any number in the thesis. Today: all checks pass, 230 figure mentions agree.
  5. python test_reward.py · 1 minute
    All four checks must pass, and the neutral policy must score −0.00438. A training curve computed against a broken reward is worse than no curve, because it looks like progress.

Rules that are never broken

  • The project never writes to the vehicle's ECU. Read-only OBD-II logging. No flashing, no CAN transmission, no tuning. If a task seems to require writing to the car, it is the wrong task — say so rather than finding a way.
  • Never edit data/ or logs/raw/ by hand. The first is generated; the second is measurement.
  • Never quote a number that no script prints.
  • Never change the evaluation set after seeing results. Twenty episodes are fixed once and treated as read-only. This is the one mistake the project cannot recover from.
Appendix B
Week 2 · teach-backs

Five people, five concepts

In an individual defence, "that was someone else's part" is a bad answer. Each member takes one concept and teaches it to the other four in ten minutes.

Knock
The constraint everything else in the project defends. Understand why it breaks pistons and you understand why the engine cannot simply make peak torque.
Wiebe and MBT
How a spark angle becomes a heat-release schedule, and why the best angle for torque is not always available.
Lambda and enrichment
Why the engine deliberately burns extra fuel, and why that is a thermal decision rather than a power one.
The thermal network
Three nodes, a heat capacity and a time constant. This node is where the whole idea of the project lives.
Constrained MDP
Why we use a constraint rather than a weight. "How much fuel is one missed newton-metre worth?" has no defensible answer.
How do you know the simulator is right?
Two independent checks. Against published combustion figures: 8 of 11 quantities inside band, and we report the three misses with reasons rather than tuning to close them. Against the car: a 1.4 % load residual over 22 operating points drawn from 175.5 minutes of logging.
And read mistake 12 before quoting the second one: the residual does not test the breathing model. Say that yourself before the examiner does.
Isn't predictive engine control already done?
Yes, and we say so on the prior-art slide. We are not proposing a controller. We are measuring when preview information is worth acquiring — the question that comes before building one.
How do you know the advantage comes from preview and not from a better agent?
This is the strongest answer we have. We run the same policy with the preview channels zeroed. The blinded policy lands on the reactive policy's score to the decimal — 548.6 against 548.6 — and that identity has held across two engines, two scenarios and two protection thresholds.
What would have falsified your claim?
Preview value failing to collapse onto one curve against H/τ when plotted from two unrelated plants. We have already refuted one of our own sub-hypotheses and kept the result, so the answer is not hypothetical.

Every number on this page is printed by a script in the repository. The traces in the race widget are not an illustration — they are the output of check_premise.py itself, four full rollouts over 720 seconds, taken on 13 September 2026. The spark and lambda curves are plant.predict() output directly.

The published figures were verified with python verify_docs.py on the same day: all checks pass, 230 figure mentions across the tracked files agree with the data, and none of the retired figures appears as a live claim.

If this page disagrees with the script, the script is right and this page needs rebuilding.
